Under Assumption 2, conditional on the past and current joint state at epoch \(m\), the action has behavior probabilities \(b(\cdot\mid X_m)\). If \(b(a\mid x)=0\), then \(0\le e(a\mid x)\le Lb(a\mid x)=0\), hence \(e(a\mid x)=0\); therefore, for every observed state \(x\), \[\sum_{a\in\mathcal A} b(a\mid x)\, \begin{cases} 0, & b(a\mid x)=0,\\[1mm] e(a\mid x)/b(a\mid x), & b(a\mid x)\ne 0 \end{cases} = \sum_{a\in\mathcal A}e(a\mid x)=1.\] Thus, for every \(m\in\{r,\ldots,t\}\), conditioning on the history and current joint state up to epoch \(m\) gives \[\mathbb E\!\left[\left.\rho_m(\tau)\,\right|\, X_{0:m},H_{0:m},A_{0:m-1},Y_{0:m-1}\right]=1.\] Peeling the factors one epoch at a time, with Assumption 1 identifying the next history law after each peeled action, gives \[\mathbb E\!\left[\prod_{j=r}^{m}\rho_j(\tau)\right] = \mathbb E\!\left[\prod_{j=r}^{m-1}\rho_j(\tau)\right], \qquad m=r,\ldots,t,\] where the product over \(\{r,\ldots,r-1\}\) is the empty product. Iterating from \(m=t\) down to \(m=r\) leaves the empty product, whose expectation is \(1\), and hence \[\mathbb E\!\left[\prod_{j=r}^{t}\rho_j(\tau)\right]=1.\] It remains to pass this identity to observed trajectories. Let \(O\) be the observation map \[O(\tau)=((X_j(\tau),A_j(\tau),Y_j(\tau)))_{j=0}^{T-1},\] so that the observed law is the pushforward of the full law by \(O\). By definition of \(\Pi_{t,k}\) and the interval identity \(\mathcal W_{t,k}=\{r, \ldots,t\}\), for every full trajectory \(\tau\), \[\Pi_{t,k}(O(\tau)) = \prod_{j\in\mathcal W_{t,k}}\rho_j(O(\tau)) = \prod_{j=r}^{t}\rho_j(\tau).\] The usual integration formula for a pushforward measure therefore gives \[\mathbb E_{\mathrm{obs}}\bigl[\Pi_{t,k}\bigr] = \int \Pi_{t,k}(o)\,d\mathbb P_{\mathrm{obs}}(o) = \int \Pi_{t,k}(O(\tau))\,d\mathbb P(\tau) = \int \prod_{j=r}^{t}\rho_j(\tau)\,d\mathbb P(\tau) =1.\]

  3. For every observed trajectory \(o\) and every \(j\), \[0\le \rho_j(o)\le L.\] The lower bound uses the behavior-policy vector property from Assumption 2 and the theorem hypothesis that the target carrier is a policy vector. The upper bound is immediate in a zero cell and otherwise follows from the domination inequality in Assumption 5 by dividing \(e(a_j\mid x_j)\le Lb(a_j\mid x_j)\) by the positive number \(b(a_j\mid x_j)\). Hence \[0\le \Pi_{t,k}(o)\le L^{|\mathcal W_{t,k}|}.\] The map \(j\mapsto t-j\) injects \(\mathcal W_{t,k}\) into \(\{0,\ldots,k\}\), so \[|\mathcal W_{t,k}|\le k+1.\] Since \(L\ge 1\), \[0\le \Pi_{t,k}(o)\le L^{k+1}.\]

  4. By Assumption 4, the full trajectory law has \(|Y_t|\le 1\) almost surely; pushing forward to the observed law gives \[|y_t|\le 1 \qquad \mathbb P_{\mathrm{obs}}\text{-almost surely}.\] Together with the preceding product bound, this also gives \[|Z_t(k)|\le L^{k+1} \qquad \mathbb P_{\mathrm{obs}}\text{-almost surely},\] so the square and the comparison function \(L^{k+1}\Pi_{t,k}\) are integrable under the observed law.

  5. For \(\mathbb P_{\mathrm{obs}}\)-almost every observed trajectory, \[Z_t(k)^2 = y_t^2\Pi_{t,k}^2 \le 1\cdot \Pi_{t,k}^2 \le L^{k+1}\Pi_{t,k},\] where the last inequality uses \(0\le \Pi_{t,k}\le L^{k+1}\). Integrating and using the normalization from step 2, \[\mathbb E_{\mathrm{obs}}\bigl[Z_t(k)^2\bigr] \le \mathbb E_{\mathrm{obs}}\bigl[L^{k+1}\Pi_{t,k}\bigr] = L^{k+1}\mathbb E_{\mathrm{obs}}\bigl[\Pi_{t,k}\bigr] = L^{k+1}.\]

∎
Lemma 9 [lem:phiw-overlapping-window-covariance] (Overlapping-window covariance).

Let \(M\) be a raw POMDP experiment of length \(T\) on finite observed and hidden alphabets. Let \(k,h\in\mathbb N\), let \(L\in\mathbb R\), and let \(t,u\in\{0,\ldots,T-1\}\). Assume:

  • (Model conditions.) \(M\) satisfies Assumption 2, Assumption 5 with overlap constant \(L\), Assumption 1, and Assumption 4.

  • (Overlap constant.) \(L\ge 1\).

  • (Initial window.) \(k\le t\).

  • (Lag identity.) \(u=t+h\).

  • (Overlapping lag.) \(1\le h\le k\).

Then the depth-\(k\) scores \(Z_t(k)\) of Definition 16, evaluated under the observed trajectory law of \(M\), satisfy \[\bigl|\operatorname{Cov}_{\mathrm{obs}}\bigl(Z_t(k),Z_u(k)\bigr)\bigr| \le 2L^{k+1-h}.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 9.

  1. Let \[\mu_{\mathrm{obs}}:=\mathcal L_{\mathrm{obs}}(M)\] be the observed-trajectory law of \(M\). Since it is the pushforward of the probability law of the full raw experiment under the observation map, \(\mu_{\mathrm{obs}}\) is a probability measure. Also, from \(k\le t\), \(u=t+h\), and \(h\ge 1\), we have \[k\le u .\]

  2. We use the following covariance reduction. If \(\mu\) is a probability law, \(\xi,\eta\in L^2(\mu)\), and a number \(B\) satisfies \[\int |\xi|\,d\mu\le 1,\qquad \int |\eta|\,d\mu\le 1,\qquad \int |\xi\eta|\,d\mu\le B,\qquad 1\le B,\] then \[|\operatorname{Cov}_{\mu}(\xi,\eta)|\le 2B .\] Indeed, \[|\operatorname{Cov}_{\mu}(\xi,\eta)| \le \int |\xi\eta|\,d\mu + \left(\int |\xi|\,d\mu\right) \left(\int |\eta|\,d\mu\right),\] by expanding the covariance and applying the triangle inequality. The two \(L^1\)-bounds give \[\left(\int |\xi|\,d\mu\right) \left(\int |\eta|\,d\mu\right)\le 1,\] because both factors are nonnegative. Hence \[|\operatorname{Cov}_{\mu}(\xi,\eta)| \le B+1\le 2B .\]

  3. Apply this reduction with \[\xi(w):=Z_t(k)(w), \qquad \eta(w):=Z_u(k)(w), \qquad B:=L^{k+1-h},\] for observed trajectories \(w\). The two scores are square-integrable under \(\mu_{\mathrm{obs}}\). To see this, write the one-step ratio in Definition 16 as \[\rho_j(w) := \begin{cases} e(A_j(w)\mid X_j(w))/b(A_j(w)\mid X_j(w)),& b(A_j(w)\mid X_j(w))>0,\\ 0,& b(A_j(w)\mid X_j(w))=0, \end{cases}\] for every observed trajectory \(w\). The policy-vector parts of Assumptions 2 and 5 give \(b(a\mid x),e(a\mid x)\ge 0\). If \(b(a\mid x)>0\), Assumption 5 gives \[0\le {e(a\mid x)\over b(a\mid x)}\le L .\] If \(b(a\mid x)=0\), then \(e(a\mid x)\le Lb(a\mid x)=0\), hence \(e(a\mid x)=0\), and the zero-cell convention sets the ratio equal to zero. Thus, for every observed trajectory \(w\), \[0\le \rho_j(w)\le L \qquad\text{for every }j .\] Also \(|Y_r|\le 1\) almost surely by Assumption 4. Hence, for every time \(r\), \[|Z_r(k)(w)| \le \prod_{j\in\mathcal W_r}\rho_j(w) \le L^{|\mathcal W_r|} \le L^{k+1}, \qquad \mathcal W_r:=\{j: j\le r,\ r-j\le k\},\] outside the null set where the reward bound fails. The window \(\mathcal W_r\) is a subset of the integer interval \(\{r-k,\ldots,r\}\), so \(|\mathcal W_r|\le k+1\). The map \(w\mapsto Z_r(k)(w)\) is measurable: the observed trajectory space carries real rewards and is not finite, so measurability is not a counting argument, but each coordinate map \(w\mapsto(X_j(w),A_j(w))\) and \(w\mapsto Y_j(w)\) is measurable, the ratio \(\rho_j\) is a measurable function of the first of these by the two-case definition above, and \(Z_r(k)\) is a finite product of these measurable maps. Since \(\mu_{\mathrm{obs}}\) is a probability law, the almost-sure bound above implies \(Z_r(k)\in L^2(\mu_{\mathrm{obs}})\), and in particular it applies to \(r=t\) and \(r=u\).

    Moreover, Assumption 1 and the initial-window conditions \(k\le t\) and \(k\le u\) give \[\int |Z_t(k)|\,d\mu_{\mathrm{obs}}\le 1, \qquad \int |Z_u(k)|\,d\mu_{\mathrm{obs}}\le 1 .\] For a single time \(r\) with \(k\le r\), this last estimate is obtained as follows. For integers \(r_{\mathrm{lo}},r_{\mathrm{hi}}\) with \(0\le r_{\mathrm{lo}}\le r_{\mathrm{hi}}<T\), define \[\Gamma_{r_{\mathrm{lo}},r_{\mathrm{hi}}}(w) := \prod_{j=r_{\mathrm{lo}}}^{r_{\mathrm{hi}}}\rho_j(w),\] with the empty product equal to one when the lower endpoint is above the upper endpoint. This chronological block has unit expectation: \[\int \Gamma_{r_{\mathrm{lo}},r_{\mathrm{hi}}}(w)\,d\mu_{\mathrm{obs}}(w)=1 .\] To prove this, pull the integral back from \(\mu_{\mathrm{obs}}\) to the full trajectory law, so that \(X_j,A_j\), and \(Y_j\) are full-trajectory coordinates. For \(s\in\{r_{\mathrm{lo}},\ldots,r_{\mathrm{hi}}\}\), let \[\mathscr G_s := \sigma(X_0,H_0,A_0,Y_0,\ldots,A_{s-1},Y_{s-1},X_s,H_s)\] and \[\Gamma^-_{r_{\mathrm{lo}},s} := \prod_{j=r_{\mathrm{lo}}}^{s-1}\rho_j,\] where the product is one for \(s=r_{\mathrm{lo}}\). The factor \(\Gamma^-_{r_{\mathrm{lo}},s}\) is \(\mathscr G_s\)-measurable. By Assumption 2, conditionally on \(\mathscr G_s\) the action \(A_s\) has law \(b(\cdot\mid X_s)\). The zero-cell convention and the implication \(b(a\mid x)=0\Rightarrow e(a\mid x)=0\) computed above give \[\sum_{a\in\mathcal A} b(a\mid X_s)\rho(a,X_s) = \sum_{a\in\mathcal A} e(a\mid X_s) = 1,\] and hence \[\mathbb E\!\left[ \Gamma^-_{r_{\mathrm{lo}},s}\rho_s \mid \mathscr G_s \right] = \Gamma^-_{r_{\mathrm{lo}},s} .\] The remaining coordinates after the action at time \(s\) are integrated with the probability kernel in Assumption 1; integrating first over the fibre containing \((Y_s,X_{s+1},H_{s+1})\) preserves the expectation of any function of \(\mathscr G_s\). Applying the displayed conditional identity successively for \(s=r_{\mathrm{hi}},r_{\mathrm{hi}}-1,\ldots,r_{\mathrm{lo}}\) removes the factors one at a time and leaves the empty product, whose expectation is one. Taking \(r_{\mathrm{lo}}=r-k\) and \(r_{\mathrm{hi}}=r\) gives \[\int \prod_{j=r-k}^{r}\rho_j(w)\,d\mu_{\mathrm{obs}}(w)=1 .\] Now \[Z_r(k)(w) = Y_r(w)\prod_{j=r-k}^{r}\rho_j(w),\] and \(|Y_r|\le 1\) almost surely by Assumption 4. Therefore \[\int |Z_r(k)|\,d\mu_{\mathrm{obs}} \le \int \prod_{j=r-k}^{r}\rho_j(w)\,d\mu_{\mathrm{obs}}(w) = 1 .\]

  4. It remains to bound the cross moment. For the two overlapping windows define \[\mathcal W_t:=\{j\in\{0,\ldots,T-1\}: t-k\le j\le t\}, \qquad \mathcal W_u:=\{j\in\{0,\ldots,T-1\}: u-k\le j\le u\}, \qquad \mathcal W_{\cup}:=\mathcal W_t\cup\mathcal W_u .\] Since \(t\) and \(u\) are elements of \(\{0,\ldots,T-1\}\), their integer values are valid finite-time endpoints. The assumptions \(k\le t\), \(u=t+h\), and \(h\le k\) give \(t-k\le u\) and identify the union in the formal finite index set as \[\mathcal W_t\cup\mathcal W_u = \{j\in\{0,\ldots,T-1\}: t-k\le j\le u\}.\] The same endpoint inequalities give the cardinality bound for the overlap, \[|\mathcal W_t\cap\mathcal W_u|\le k+1-h .\] Using the same zero-cell ratio \(\rho_j\) as above, the policy-vector and overlap parts of Assumptions 2 and 5, together with \(L\ge 1\), give \[0\le \rho_j(w)\le L \qquad\text{for every }j,w .\] Therefore \[\prod_{j\in\mathcal W_t\cap\mathcal W_u}\rho_j(w) \le L^{|\mathcal W_t\cap\mathcal W_u|} \le L^{k+1-h},\] the second inequality by the cardinality bound \(|\mathcal W_t\cap\mathcal W_u|\le k+1-h\) together with the monotonicity of \(n\mapsto L^{n}\) for \(L\ge1\); the intersection need not have exactly \(k+1-h\) indices, so this step is an inequality and not an identity.

  5. Since \(|Y_t|\le 1\) and \(|Y_u|\le 1\) almost surely, \[|Z_t(k)Z_u(k)| \le \left(\prod_{j\in\mathcal W_t}\rho_j\right) \left(\prod_{j\in\mathcal W_u}\rho_j\right).\] The product over two windows decomposes into the product over the union times the product over the overlap: \[\left(\prod_{j\in\mathcal W_t}\rho_j\right) \left(\prod_{j\in\mathcal W_u}\rho_j\right) = \left(\prod_{j\in\mathcal W_{\cup}}\rho_j\right) \left(\prod_{j\in\mathcal W_t\cap\mathcal W_u}\rho_j\right).\] Combining this with the previous display gives the pointwise almost-sure bound \[|Z_t(k)Z_u(k)| \le L^{k+1-h} \prod_{j\in\mathcal W_{\cup}}\rho_j .\] The finite-window identity from the preceding step rewrites the union product in exactly chronological form: \[\mathcal W_{\cup} = \{j\in\{0,\ldots,T-1\}: t-k\le j\le u\}, \qquad \prod_{j\in\mathcal W_{\cup}}\rho_j(w) = \prod_{j=t-k}^{u}\rho_j(w).\] The interval-normalization argument proved above applies with lower endpoint \(t-k\) and upper endpoint \(u\). The lower endpoint is nonnegative because \(k\le t\), the upper endpoint is in \(\{0,\ldots,T-1\}\) because \(u\) is a time index, and \(t-k\le u\) follows from \(u=t+h\). Thus \[\int \prod_{j\in\mathcal W_{\cup}}\rho_j(w)\,d\mu_{\mathrm{obs}}(w) = \int \prod_{j=t-k}^{u}\rho_j(w)\,d\mu_{\mathrm{obs}}(w) = 1 .\] Hence \[\int |Z_t(k)Z_u(k)|\,d\mu_{\mathrm{obs}} \le L^{k+1-h}.\]

  6. Finally, \[1\le L^{k+1-h},\] because \(L\ge 1\). The covariance reduction from Step 2, applied with \(B=L^{k+1-h}\), yields \[\bigl|\operatorname{Cov}_{\mathrm{obs}}(Z_t(k),Z_u(k))\bigr| \le 2L^{k+1-h},\] which is the claimed bound.

∎

The analysis of separated windows uses both the observed law and the full trajectory law. The next definitions fix those probability spaces and the observation map connecting them.

Definition 26 [synth_15] (Observed-trajectory law).

For a raw POMDP experiment \(M=(\mathcal X,\mathcal H,K,b,e,\mathcal L(\mathsf O_T))\), let \[\mathbb P_M^{\mathrm{obs}}:=\operatorname{Law}^{\mathrm{obs}}_M\] denote the probability law of the observed trajectory \[\mathsf O_T=((X_t,A_t,Y_t))_{t=0}^{T-1}\] induced by the behavior-policy experiment \(M\). Equivalently, under \(\mathbb P_M^{\mathrm{obs}}\), \(S_0\sim d_b\), \(A_t\sim b(\cdot\mid X_t)\), and \(K\) generates \((Y_t,S_{t+1})\) from \((S_t,A_t)\), with only \((X_t,A_t,Y_t)_{t=0}^{T-1}\) retained.

Definition 27 [synth_17] (Full trajectory law and observation map).

For a raw POMDP experiment \(M=(\mathcal X,\mathcal H,K,b,e,\mathcal L(\mathsf O_T))\) with joint state space \(\mathcal S=\mathcal X\times\mathcal H\), let \(\mathbb P_M\) denote the full behavior-policy trajectory law on \[(S_0,A_0,Y_0,S_1,\ldots,A_{T-1},Y_{T-1},S_T).\] Under \(\mathbb P_M\), the initial state satisfies \(S_0\sim d_b\), each action is drawn as \(A_t\sim b(\cdot\mid X_t)\), and the kernel \(K\) generates \((Y_t,S_{t+1})\) from \((S_t,A_t)\), for \(t=0,\ldots,T-1\).

Let \(O\) be the observation map from a full trajectory to its observed trajectory, \[O(S_0,A_0,Y_0,\ldots,A_{T-1},Y_{T-1},S_T) :=((X_t,A_t,Y_t))_{t=0}^{T-1},\] where \(S_t=(X_t,H_t)\). The observed behavior-policy law is the pushforward \[\mathbb P_M^{\mathrm{obs}}:=\mathbb P_M\circ O^{-1}.\]

Together, Definitions 26 and 27 let the covariance calculation move between observable scores and latent-state transition operators. The observed law is the sampling law for the estimator, while the full law retains the latent state needed to apply contraction after a gap.

Lemma 10 [lem:phiw-separated-window-peeling] (Separated window peeling).

Let \(M\) be a raw POMDP experiment with trajectory length \(T\), and let \(L\), the policy-overlap constant, be real. Let \(Z_s(k)\) denote the depth-\(k\) partial-history weighted reward score from Definition 16. Suppose:

  • (Model conditions.) \(M\) satisfies Assumptions 2, 5, 1, and 4 with overlap constant \(L\).

  • (Overlap scale.) \(1\le L\).

  • (Mature earlier window.) The history depth \(k\) satisfies \(k\le t\).

  • (Separated future window.) For an epoch \(t\) and a gap \(\gamma\in\mathbb N\), the epoch \[u=t+1+\gamma+k\] lies in the observed horizon, equivalently \(t+1+\gamma+k<T\).

For \(p\in\{b,e\}\), let \(P_p\) be the policy-induced transition operator obtained from the reward-marginalized kernel under policy \(p\), let \[g_e(s)=\sum_a e(a\mid x(s))\int y\,K(dy\times\mathcal S\mid s,a),\] and put \[\Phi_{\gamma,k}(s)=P_b^\gamma P_e^k g_e(s).\] Then, with \(\mathbb P_M^{\mathrm{obs}}\) the observed law induced by the observed trajectory map \(O\), \[\int Z_t(k)(w)Z_u(k)(w)\,d\mathbb P_M^{\mathrm{obs}}(w) = \int Z_t(k)(O(\tau))\,\Phi_{\gamma,k}(S_{t+1}(\tau))\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau).\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 10.

Set \[\iota_0=t+1,\qquad \iota_1=t+1+\gamma,\qquad \iota_2=\iota_1+k=u .\] The horizon condition gives \(\iota_2<T\), and \(k\le t\) makes the earlier score mature. For a full trajectory \(\tau\), write the joint state at epoch \(m\) as \[S_m(\tau):=(X_m(\tau),H_m(\tau))\] and write \[\operatorname{hist}_m(\tau) = \bigl((S_j(\tau))_{j<m},(A_j(\tau),Y_j(\tau))_{j<m},S_m(\tau)\bigr)\] for the state-and-epoch history through epoch \(m\). For an observed trajectory \(w\), write its epoch-\(j\) coordinates as \[w_j=(x_j(w),a_j(w),y_j(w)).\] Define the totalized one-step ratio, for an observed state \(x\) and action \(a\), by \[\rho(a\mid x) := \begin{cases} 0, & b(a\mid x)=0,\\ e(a\mid x)/b(a\mid x), & b(a\mid x)>0, \end{cases} \qquad \rho_j(w):=\rho(a_j(w)\mid x_j(w)).\] For \(0\le j_{\mathrm{lo}}\le j_{\mathrm{hi}}\le T-1\), put \[\operatorname{rat}^{<}_{j_{\mathrm{lo}}:j_{\mathrm{hi}}}(\tau) := \prod_{j=j_{\mathrm{lo}}}^{j_{\mathrm{hi}}-1}\rho_j(O(\tau)), \qquad \operatorname{rat}^{<}_{j_{\mathrm{hi}}:j_{\mathrm{hi}}}(\tau):=1 .\] Thus the superscript \({<}\) means that the upper endpoint is excluded: the largest ratio in a nonempty block is \(\rho_{j_{\mathrm{hi}}-1}\). In particular, \(\operatorname{rat}^{<}_{\iota_1:\iota_2}\) stops at \(\rho_{u-1}\), while the terminal factor \(\rho_u\) is written separately below. The target-policy one-step reward regression is \[g_e(s)= \sum_{a\in\mathcal A}e(a\mid x(s)) \int y\,K(dy\times\mathcal S\mid s,a),\] and the separated future function is \(\Phi_{\gamma,k}=P_b^\gamma P_e^k g_e\). In this proof the policy-induced transition operator is the reward-marginalized kernel \[(P_pF)(s) = \sum_{a\in\mathcal A}p(a\mid x(s)) \int F(s')\,K(dy\times ds'\mid s,a), \qquad p\in\{b,e\},\] for every bounded \(F:\mathcal S\to\mathbb R\).

  1. Since \(\mathbb P_M^{\mathrm{obs}}\) is the pushforward of \(\mathbb P_M\) under \(O\), \[\int Z_t(k)(w)Z_u(k)(w)\,d\mathbb P_M^{\mathrm{obs}}(w) = \int Z_t(k)(O(\tau))Z_u(k)(O(\tau))\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau).\] The measurability needed for this change of variables follows from the observable definition of \(Z_t(k)\) in Definition 16.

  2. Define lifted carriers on state histories by \[\Lambda_0(\operatorname{hist}_{\iota_0}(\tau)) :=Z_t(k)(O(\tau)),\] and, for \(0\le \nu<\gamma+k\), let \(\Lambda_{\nu+1}\) be the lift of \(\Lambda_\nu\) that forgets the last appended action, reward, and next state: \[\Lambda_{\nu+1}(\operatorname{hist}_{\iota_0+\nu+1}(\tau)) := \Lambda_\nu(\operatorname{hist}_{\iota_0+\nu}(\tau)).\] The base carrier is well defined because \(Z_t(k)\) uses only rewards, actions, and observed states through epoch \(t\), while \(t<\iota_0\); hence every lifted carrier satisfies \[\Lambda_\nu(\operatorname{hist}_{\iota_0+\nu}(\tau)) = Z_t(k)(O(\tau)) \qquad(0\le \nu\le \gamma+k).\] The depth-\(k\) window in \(Z_u(k)\) is inclusive. Since \(u=\iota_2\) and \(u-k=\iota_1\), the half-open block contains the factors with \(\iota_1\le j<\iota_2\), and the endpoint contributes the separate terminal factor: \[Z_u(k)(O(\tau)) = Y_{\iota_2}(\tau)\, \operatorname{rat}^{<}_{\iota_1:\iota_2}(\tau)\, \rho_{\iota_2}(O(\tau)).\] Therefore \[\int Z_t(k)(O(\tau))Z_u(k)(O(\tau))\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau) = \int \Lambda_{\gamma+k}(\operatorname{hist}_{\iota_2}(\tau))\, \operatorname{rat}^{<}_{\iota_1:\iota_2}(\tau)\, \rho_{\iota_2}(O(\tau))Y_{\iota_2}(\tau)\, d\mathbb P_M(\tau).\] The totalized ratios obey \(0\le\rho(a\mid x)\le L\): on a positive behavior cell this is the domination inequality divided by \(b(a\mid x)>0\), and on a zero cell it follows from the definition of \(\rho\). Hence finite state spaces, Assumption 4, and the finite product bound \(\prod_j\rho_j\le L^{\#\{j\}}\) make all displayed integrands integrable. Empty behavior or target blocks are interpreted as products equal to one; in particular, when \(k=0\) the block \(\operatorname{rat}^{<}_{\iota_1:\iota_2}\) is empty and the endpoint factor \(\rho_{\iota_2}(O(\tau))\) is still present.

  3. The terminal reward peel is \[\begin{aligned} &\int \Lambda_{\gamma+k}(\operatorname{hist}_{\iota_2})\, \operatorname{rat}^{<}_{\iota_1:\iota_2}\, \rho_{\iota_2}Y_{\iota_2}\,d\mathbb P_M \\ &\qquad = \int \Lambda_{\gamma+k}(\operatorname{hist}_{\iota_2})\, \operatorname{rat}^{<}_{\iota_1:\iota_2}\, g_e(S_{\iota_2})\,d\mathbb P_M . \end{aligned}\] Indeed, condition first on the history and action at \(\iota_2\). Assumption 1 supplies the conditional law of \((Y_{\iota_2},S_{\iota_2+1})\) as \(K(\cdot\mid S_{\iota_2},a)\). Applying this conditional-law identity to the measurable test function \((y,s')\mapsto y\), with integrability supplied by Assumption 4, and then marginalizing over the successor state gives \[E[Y_{\iota_2}\mid \operatorname{hist}_{\iota_2},A_{\iota_2}=a] = \int y\,K(dy\times\mathcal S\mid S_{\iota_2},a).\] By Assumption 2, the action is drawn from \(b(\cdot\mid X_{\iota_2})\). For every observed state \(x\) and action \(a\), the totalized ratio satisfies \[b(a\mid x)\rho(a\mid x)=e(a\mid x).\] Indeed, if \(b(a\mid x)>0\), this is the definition of \(\rho\). If \(b(a\mid x)=0\), then the domination inequality in Assumption 5 gives \(e(a\mid x)\le0\), while the target policy has nonnegative coordinates, so \(e(a\mid x)=0\), matching the zero-cell value. Applying this identity at \(x=X_{\iota_2}\) and summing over \(a\) gives \[\sum_{a\in\mathcal A}e(a\mid X_{\iota_2}) \int y\,K(dy\times\mathcal S\mid S_{\iota_2},a) =g_e(S_{\iota_2}),\] by the displayed definition of \(g_e\).

  4. For every \(\iota_1\le m<\iota_2\), put \(\nu=m-\iota_0\), so \(\gamma\le\nu<\gamma+k\). For every bounded \(F:\mathcal S\to\mathbb R\), the target-ratio peeling step is \[\begin{aligned} &\int \Lambda_{\nu+1}(\operatorname{hist}_{m+1})\, \operatorname{rat}^{<}_{\iota_1:m+1}\, F(S_{m+1})\,d\mathbb P_M \\ &\qquad = \int \Lambda_{\nu}(\operatorname{hist}_{m})\, \operatorname{rat}^{<}_{\iota_1:m}\, (P_eF)(S_m)\,d\mathbb P_M . \end{aligned}\] The first target peel has \(m=\iota_1\) and lowers the carrier from \(\Lambda_{\gamma+1}\) to \(\Lambda_\gamma\); the last has \(m=\iota_2-1\) and lowers \(\Lambda_{\gamma+k}\) to \(\Lambda_{\gamma+k-1}\). Iterating the displayed identity backward over these values of \(m\) therefore removes exactly the \(k\) target-window ratios. Indeed, \(\Lambda_{\nu+1}\) is the lift of \(\Lambda_\nu\), and \[\operatorname{rat}^{<}_{\iota_1:m+1} = \operatorname{rat}^{<}_{\iota_1:m}\rho_m.\] Conditioning on the history and action at \(m\), Assumption 1 gives the transition integral over \((Y_m,S_{m+1})\), and the reward coordinate is integrated out: \[\sum_{a\in\mathcal A} b(a\mid X_m)\rho(a\mid X_m) \int F(s')\,K(dy\times ds'\mid S_m,a) = \sum_{a\in\mathcal A} e(a\mid X_m) \int F(s')\,K(dy\times ds'\mid S_m,a).\] The ratio identity displayed in the terminal peel justifies the replacement of \(b\rho\) by \(e\), including zero behavior cells, and the last display is exactly \((P_eF)(S_m)\). Iterating for \(m=\iota_2-1,\ldots,\iota_1\), starting from \(F=g_e\), yields \[\int \Lambda_{\gamma+k}(\operatorname{hist}_{\iota_2})\, \operatorname{rat}^{<}_{\iota_1:\iota_2}\, g_e(S_{\iota_2})\,d\mathbb P_M = \int \Lambda_{\gamma}(\operatorname{hist}_{\iota_1})\, (P_e^kg_e)(S_{\iota_1})\,d\mathbb P_M .\] When \(k=0\), this iteration is empty and the displayed identity reduces to the same integrand at \(\iota_1=\iota_2\).

  5. For every \(\iota_0\le m<\iota_1\), put \(\nu=m-\iota_0\). For every bounded \(F:\mathcal S\to\mathbb R\), the behavior-gap peeling step is \[\int \Lambda_{\nu+1}(\operatorname{hist}_{m+1})F(S_{m+1})\,d\mathbb P_M = \int \Lambda_{\nu}(\operatorname{hist}_{m})(P_bF)(S_m)\,d\mathbb P_M .\] Here \(\operatorname{hist}_{m+1}\) is the history obtained from \(\operatorname{hist}_m\) by appending \((A_m,Y_m,S_{m+1})\), and \(\Lambda_{\nu+1}\) is the lift of \(\Lambda_\nu\), so \(\Lambda_{\nu+1}(\operatorname{hist}_{m+1})=\Lambda_\nu(\operatorname{hist}_m)\). Conditioning on the history and action at \(m\), Assumption 2 supplies the behavior action law, while Assumption 1 supplies the joint law of \((Y_m,S_{m+1})\) given \((S_m,A_m)\). Integrating out the reward coordinate gives, for each history through \(m\), \[\sum_{a\in\mathcal A} b(a\mid X_m) \int F(s')\,K(dy\times ds'\mid S_m,a) = (P_bF)(S_m),\] which is the reward-marginalized behavior transition appearing in the displayed identity. Iterating over \(m=\iota_1-1,\ldots,\iota_0\) with \(F=P_e^kg_e\) gives \[\int \Lambda_{\gamma}(\operatorname{hist}_{\iota_1})(P_e^kg_e)(S_{\iota_1})\,d\mathbb P_M = \int \Lambda_{0}(\operatorname{hist}_{\iota_0}) (P_b^\gamma P_e^kg_e)(S_{\iota_0})\,d\mathbb P_M .\] When \(\gamma=0\), this iteration is empty. Since \(\Lambda_0(\operatorname{hist}_{\iota_0}(\tau))=Z_t(k)(O(\tau))\), \(S_{\iota_0}=S_{t+1}\), and \(\Phi_{\gamma,k}=P_b^\gamma P_e^kg_e\), combining the preceding displays proves \[\int Z_t(k)(w)Z_u(k)(w)\,d\mathbb P_M^{\mathrm{obs}}(w) = \int Z_t(k)(O(\tau))\,\Phi_{\gamma,k}(S_{t+1}(\tau))\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau).\]

∎

Lemma 10 rewrites a separated score product as an earlier observable score multiplied by a future continuation value evaluated at \(S_{t+1}\). This identity supplies the point where the gap between windows becomes a behavior-transition power, followed by a target-policy window.

Lemma 11 [lem:behavior-support-operator-congruence] (Behavior-support operator congruence).

Let \(M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)\) satisfy Definition 10, with behavior policy \(b\), target policy \(e\), and the policy-overlap condition in Assumption 5. For \(p\in\{b,e\}\), write \[P_p(s,s')=\sum_{a\in\{0,1\}} p(a\mid x(s))\,K(\{S'=s'\}\mid s,a),\] and let \(P_p^0 h=h\) and \(P_p^{m+1}h=P_p(P_p^m h)\). Let \(d_b\) denote the behavior stationary law of \(P_b\).

Assume:

  • (Policy choice.) The policy \(p\) is either \(b\) or \(e\).

  • (Terminal agreement.) The functions \(f,g:\mathcal S\to\mathbb R\) satisfy \[f(s)=g(s)\] for every joint state \(s\) with \(d_b(s)>0\).

  • (Initial support.) The joint state \(s\) satisfies \(d_b(s)>0\).

Then, for every \(m\in\mathbb N\), \[(P_p^m f)(s)=(P_p^m g)(s).\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 11.

Fix a policy \(p\in\{b,e\}\). The argument first records the support-closure fact used at the induction step. For joint states \(\sigma,\tau\in\mathcal S\) and an action \(a\in\{0,1\}\), write \[\kappa_{\sigma,\tau}(a) := K(\{S'=\tau\}\mid \sigma,a).\] Then \[P_p(\sigma,\tau) = \sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}p(a\mid x(\sigma))\,\kappa_{\sigma,\tau}(a).\]

  1. Suppose \(d_b(\sigma)>0\) and \(P_p(\sigma,\tau)>0\). We claim that \[d_b(\tau)>0 .\] First show that \(P_b(\sigma,\tau)>0\). If \(p=b\), this is immediate. If \(p=e\), suppose instead that \(P_b(\sigma,\tau)=0\). By Definition 10, the behavior policy is a probability vector and \(K\) is a Markov kernel, so every summand in \[P_b(\sigma,\tau) = \sum_{a\in\{0,1\}} b(a\mid x(\sigma))\,\kappa_{\sigma,\tau}(a)\] is nonnegative. Hence, for each action \(a\), \[b(a\mid x(\sigma))\,\kappa_{\sigma,\tau}(a)=0 .\] For such an \(a\), if \(\kappa_{\sigma,\tau}(a)=0\) then \[e(a\mid x(\sigma))\,\kappa_{\sigma,\tau}(a)=0.\] If \(\kappa_{\sigma,\tau}(a)>0\), the preceding zero product gives \(b(a\mid x(\sigma))=0\); by Assumption 5, \[e(a\mid x(\sigma)) \le L\,b(a\mid x(\sigma)) = 0,\] and the target policy is nonnegative by Definition 10, so \(e(a\mid x(\sigma))=0\). Thus again \[e(a\mid x(\sigma))\,\kappa_{\sigma,\tau}(a)=0 .\] Summing over \(a\) gives \[P_e(\sigma,\tau) = \sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}e(a\mid x(\sigma))\,\kappa_{\sigma,\tau}(a) = 0,\] contradicting \(P_p(\sigma,\tau)>0\) in the case \(p=e\). Therefore \(P_b(\sigma,\tau)>0\).

    By Definition 10, \(d_b\) is stationary for \(P_b\), so \[d_b(\tau) = \sum_{\omega\in\mathcal S} d_b(\omega)P_b(\omega,\tau).\] All terms in this sum are nonnegative. Therefore \[0 < d_b(\sigma)P_b(\sigma,\tau) \le \sum_{\omega\in\mathcal S} d_b(\omega)P_b(\omega,\tau) = d_b(\tau),\] which proves the claim.

  2. We now prove the asserted identity by induction on \(m\). For \(m=0\), \[(P_p^0f)(s)=f(s)=g(s)=(P_p^0g)(s),\] because \(d_b(s)>0\) and \(f=g\) on the behavior support.

    Assume the statement holds for \(m\): for every joint state \(\sigma\) with \(d_b(\sigma)>0\), \[(P_p^m f)(\sigma)=(P_p^m g)(\sigma).\] Let \(s\) satisfy \(d_b(s)>0\). Using the recursive definition of the iterated operator, \[(P_p^{m+1}f)(s) = \sum_{\tau\in\mathcal S}P_p(s,\tau)(P_p^m f)(\tau), \qquad (P_p^{m+1}g)(s) = \sum_{\tau\in\mathcal S}P_p(s,\tau)(P_p^m g)(\tau).\] Fix \(\tau\in\mathcal S\). If \(P_p(s,\tau)=0\), then the two \(\tau\)-summands are both zero. If \(P_p(s,\tau)\neq 0\), the row nonnegativity of \(P_p\) gives \(P_p(s,\tau)>0\). The support-closure claim from the previous step then gives \(d_b(\tau)>0\), so the induction hypothesis yields \[(P_p^m f)(\tau)=(P_p^m g)(\tau).\] Multiplying by \(P_p(s,\tau)\), the two \(\tau\)-summands agree. Since this holds for every \(\tau\), the sums agree: \[(P_p^{m+1}f)(s)=(P_p^{m+1}g)(s).\] The induction proves the result for every \(m\in\mathbb N\).

∎

The support congruence in Lemma 11 keeps transition-operator calculations on the behavior support. Because the overlap condition makes target transitions compatible with behavior support, functions that agree on the behavior-stationary support continue to agree after iterating either policy operator from a supported state.

Lemma 12 [lem:phiw-separated-window-covariance] (Separated window covariance).

Let \(T,n_X,n_H,k,h\in\mathbb N\), let \(t_0,\zeta,C\in\mathbb R\), and let \(M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)\) be a raw POMDP experiment as in Definition 10, with the uniform contraction restriction of Assumption 6. Write the contraction factor as \(\alpha=e^{-1/t_0}\). Let \(t,u\in\{0,\ldots,T-1\}\) satisfy \[k\le t,\qquad u=t+h,\qquad k+1\le h .\] Then the depth-\(k\) scores \(Z_t(k)\) of Definition 16, evaluated under the observed trajectory law of \(M\), satisfy \[\bigl|\operatorname{Cov}_{\mathrm{obs}}\bigl(Z_t(k),Z_u(k)\bigr)\bigr| \le 2\alpha^{h-k-1}.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 12.

Put \[\gamma:=h-k-1 .\] Then \(0\le \gamma\) by \(k+1\le h\), and \[u=t+h=t+1+\gamma+k .\] Let \(\mathbb P_M\) denote the full trajectory law and \(\mathbb P_M^{\mathrm{obs}}\) its pushforward under the observation map \(O\). For \(p\in\{b,e\}\), write \[P_p(s,s')=\sum_{a\in\mathcal A}p(a\mid x(s))K(\{S'=s'\}\mid s,a), \qquad g_e(s)=\sum_{a\in\mathcal A}e(a\mid x(s))\int y\,K(dy\times\mathcal S\mid s,a),\] and define \[\Phi_{\gamma,k}(s):=P_b^\gamma P_e^k g_e(s).\]

  1. Since \(L=\exp(\zeta)\) and \(\zeta>0\) in Definition 10, \(1\le L\). The maturity and separation hypotheses give \(t+1+\gamma+k<T\), so Lemma 10 applies and yields \[\int Z_t(k)(w)Z_u(k)(w)\,d\mathbb P_M^{\mathrm{obs}}(w) = \int Z_t(k)(O(\tau))\,\Phi_{\gamma,k}(S_{t+1}(\tau))\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau).\] For the companion mean identity, let \(r_{+}:=t+1\) and \(q_{\gamma}:=r_{+}+\gamma\), so \(u=q_{\gamma}+k\). Since \(u=q_{\gamma}+k\), the depth-\(k\) window in \(Z_u(k)\) is exactly \(q_{\gamma},q_{\gamma}+1,\ldots,u\). We claim \[\int Z_u(k)(w)\,d\mathbb P_M^{\mathrm{obs}}(w) = \int \Phi_{\gamma,k}(S_{t+1}(\tau))\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau).\] To prove this identity, condition first on \(S_{q_{\gamma}}\). Write \[\rho(a,x):= \begin{cases} e(a\mid x)/b(a\mid x),& b(a\mid x)>0,\\ 0,& b(a\mid x)=0. \end{cases}\] The zero-cell convention and Assumption 5 imply \(e(a\mid x)=0\) whenever \(b(a\mid x)=0\). Hence, for each pre-terminal epoch \(m_{\mathrm{win}}\in\{q_{\gamma},\ldots,u-1\}\), \[\sum_{a\in\mathcal A} b(a\mid x(s))\rho(a,x(s)) K(\{S'=s'\}\mid s,a) = \sum_{a\in\mathcal A} e(a\mid x(s))K(\{S'=s'\}\mid s,a) =P_e(s,s'),\] while at the terminal epoch \[\sum_{a\in\mathcal A} b(a\mid x(s))\rho(a,x(s)) \int y\,K(dy\times\mathcal S\mid s,a) = \sum_{a\in\mathcal A} e(a\mid x(s)) \int y\,K(dy\times\mathcal S\mid s,a) =g_e(s).\] Thus the target-weighted terminal window has conditional mean \(P_e^k g_e(S_{q_{\gamma}})\), with the terminal display alone applying when \(k=0\). Conditioning backward over the gap epochs \(t+1,\ldots,q_{\gamma}-1\), no score ratio appears, and Assumptions 2 and 1 give the behavior propagation \(P_b^\gamma\); when \(\gamma=0\), this is the identity operator. Bounded rewards and the finite state and action spaces justify the displayed integrals. Therefore the conditional mean given \(S_{t+1}\) is \(P_b^\gamma P_e^k g_e(S_{t+1})=\Phi_{\gamma,k}(S_{t+1})\), proving the claim. Also, by definition of \(\mathbb P_M^{\mathrm{obs}}\), \[\int Z_t(k)(w)\,d\mathbb P_M^{\mathrm{obs}}(w) = \int Z_t(k)(O(\tau))\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau).\] Thus the covariance equals the centered full-law product \[\operatorname{Cov}_{\mathrm{obs}}(Z_t(k),Z_u(k)) = \int Z_t(k)(O(\tau))\,\Phi_{\gamma,k}(S_{t+1}(\tau))\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau) - \Bigl(\int Z_t(k)(O(\tau))\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau)\Bigr) \Bigl(\int \Phi_{\gamma,k}(S_{t+1}(\tau))\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau)\Bigr).\] The covariance identity is legitimate because \[Z_t(k),Z_u(k)\in L^2(\mathbb P_M^{\mathrm{obs}}).\] Indeed, write the window product in \(Z_m(k)\) as \[R_m^{(k)}(w):=\prod_{\substack{j\in\{0,\ldots,T-1\}:\, j\le m,\ m-j\le k}} \rho_j(w), \qquad m\in\{t,u\}.\] The product contains at most \(k+1\) factors. The zero-cell convention and Assumption 5 make each factor nonnegative and at most \(L\) on observed behavior-support cells, while Assumption 4 gives \(|Y_m|\le 1\) almost surely. Hence \(|Z_m(k)|\le L^{k+1}\) almost surely under \(\mathbb P_M^{\mathrm{obs}}\), and the two scores are square-integrable.

  2. The first score has unit \(L^1\) norm: \[\int |Z_t(k)(O(\tau))|\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau)\le 1 .\] Indeed, with \[R_t^{(k)}(w):=\prod_{j=t-k}^{t}\rho_j(w),\] the zero-cell convention and Assumption 5 give \[\mathbb E_{\mathrm{obs}}\!\left[R_t^{(k)}\right]=1\] by peeling the ratios from \(j=t\) down to \(j=t-k\): conditionally at each peeled epoch, \[\sum_{a\in\mathcal A} b(a\mid X_j)\rho_j(a,X_j) = \sum_{a:\,b(a\mid X_j)>0} e(a\mid X_j) = \sum_{a\in\mathcal A} e(a\mid X_j) =1 .\] By Assumption 4, \(|Z_t(k)|\le R_t^{(k)}\) almost surely, which proves the displayed \(L^1\) bound after passing between the observed law and the full law by the observation map.

  3. Let \(d_b\) be the stationary law of \(P_b\), and define the support-truncated regression on all of \(\mathcal S\) by \[\widetilde g_e(s):= \begin{cases} g_e(s),& d_b(s)>0,\\ 0,& d_b(s)=0. \end{cases}\] For every \(s\) with \(d_b(s)>0\), \[|g_e(s)|\le 1 .\] To see this, fix such an \(s\). For an action \(a\) with \(b(a\mid x(s))>0\), consider epoch \(0\). The history before epoch \(0\) is the unique empty history, so the full history-action singleton with current state \(s\) and action \(a\) has probability \[d_b(s)b(a\mid x(s))>0,\] by Assumption 3 and the epoch-zero action factorization in Assumption 2. Under the history-next-pair factorization supplied by Assumption 1, Assumption 4 gives, for almost every history-action pair, a kernel row whose reward coordinate is almost surely in \([-1,1]\). Since this epoch-zero singleton has positive mass, that almost-sure row statement holds at \((s,a)\), and hence \[\left|\int y\,K(dy\times\mathcal S\mid s,a)\right|\le 1 .\] If \(e(a\mid x(s))>0\), then Assumption 5 forces \(b(a\mid x(s))>0\). Therefore \[|g_e(s)| \le \sum_{a\in\mathcal A} e(a\mid x(s)) \left|\int y\,K(dy\times\mathcal S\mid s,a)\right| \le \sum_{a\in\mathcal A} e(a\mid x(s)) =1.\] For \(d_b(s)=0\), the definition gives \(|\widetilde g_e(s)|=0\). Consequently \[\sup_{s,s'}|\widetilde g_e(s)-\widetilde g_e(s')|\le 2 .\]

  4. Since \(t_0>0\), \(\alpha=\exp(-1/t_0)\) satisfies \(0\le \alpha\le 1\). For a function \(f:\mathcal S\to\mathbb R\), write \[\operatorname{osc}(f):=\sup_{s,s'\in\mathcal S}|f(s)-f(s')|.\] For each \(p_\star\in\{b,e\}\), the rows of \(P_{p_\star}\) are probability vectors. Nonnegativity follows from the nonnegativity of the policy probabilities and of the kernel rows. For the row sums, \[\sum_{s'\in\mathcal S}P_{p_\star}(s,s') = \sum_{a\in\mathcal A}p_\star(a\mid x(s)) \sum_{s'\in\mathcal S}K(\{S'=s'\}\mid s,a) = \sum_{a\in\mathcal A}p_\star(a\mid x(s)) =1.\] Here Assumption 1 supplies the kernel row mass, Assumption 2 supplies the behavior probability vector when \(p_\star=b\), and the target-carrier part of Assumption 5 supplies the target probability vector when \(p_\star=e\).

    We first convert the total-variation contraction in Assumption 6 into the oscillation contraction used below. Let \(\nu\) and \(\nu'\) be probability vectors on the finite state space, and let \(f:\mathcal S\to\mathbb R\). Put \[f_{\min}:=\min_{x\in\mathcal S} f(x), \qquad f_0(x):=f(x)-f_{\min}.\] Then \(0\le f_0(x)\le \operatorname{osc}(f)\) for every \(x\), and, since \(\nu\) and \(\nu'\) have equal total mass, \[\sum_{x\in\mathcal S} f(x)(\nu(x)-\nu'(x)) = \sum_{x\in\mathcal S} f_0(x)(\nu(x)-\nu'(x)).\] The layer-cake identity on the finite state space gives \[\sum_{x\in\mathcal S} f_0(x)(\nu(x)-\nu'(x)) = \int_{0}^{\operatorname{osc}(f)} \bigl(\nu\{x:f_0(x)>\lambda_{\mathrm{lev}}\} -\nu'\{x:f_0(x)>\lambda_{\mathrm{lev}}\}\bigr) \,d\lambda_{\mathrm{lev}},\] and therefore \[\left| \sum_{x\in\mathcal S} f(x)(\nu(x)-\nu'(x)) \right| \le \operatorname{osc}(f)\,\|\nu-\nu'\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.\] Now fix states \(s,s'\). Applying Assumption 6 to the point masses \(\delta_s\) and \(\delta_{s'}\) gives \[\|\delta_s P_{p_\star}-\delta_{s'}P_{p_\star}\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \le \alpha\,\|\delta_s-\delta_{s'}\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \le \alpha .\] Combining this with the preceding dual bound yields the one-step estimate \[|P_{p_\star}f(s)-P_{p_\star}f(s')| \le \alpha\,\operatorname{osc}(f).\] Taking the supremum over \(s,s'\) and iterating gives the finite-state oscillation form: whenever \(\operatorname{osc}(f)\le B_{\mathrm{osc}}\) for some \(B_{\mathrm{osc}}\ge0\), \[\operatorname{osc}(P_{p_\star}^{m_{\mathrm{iter}}}f) \le \alpha^{m_{\mathrm{iter}}}B_{\mathrm{osc}}, \qquad m_{\mathrm{iter}}\in\mathbb N.\] Applying this first with \(p_\star=e\), \(f=\widetilde g_e\), and \(B_{\mathrm{osc}}=2\), and then using \(\alpha^k\le1\), gives \[\sup_{s,s'}|P_e^k\widetilde g_e(s)-P_e^k\widetilde g_e(s')|\le 2.\] Applying it next with \(p_\star=b\) to \(P_e^k\widetilde g_e\) gives \[\sup_{s,s'} \left| P_b^\gamma P_e^k\widetilde g_e(s) - P_b^\gamma P_e^k\widetilde g_e(s') \right| \le 2\alpha^\gamma .\]

  5. Define \[\widetilde\Phi_{\gamma,k}(s):=P_b^\gamma P_e^k\widetilde g_e(s).\] Because \(g_e=\widetilde g_e\) on \(\{s:d_b(s)>0\}\), Lemma 11 applied with policy \(e\) gives, for every behavior-supported state \(x\), \[P_e^k g_e(x)=P_e^k\widetilde g_e(x) \qquad\text{whenever }d_b(x)>0.\] This pointwise behavior-supported equality is the terminal-agreement hypothesis needed for the second application of Lemma 11, now with policy \(b\). Thus, for every initial state \(s\) with \(d_b(s)>0\), \[\Phi_{\gamma,k}(s)=P_b^\gamma P_e^k g_e(s) =P_b^\gamma P_e^k\widetilde g_e(s) =\widetilde\Phi_{\gamma,k}(s).\] We next record the behavior-stationarity identity used at time \(t+1\). For every bounded \(F:\mathcal S\to\mathbb R\) and every epoch \(m_{\mathrm{time}}\), \[\int F(S_{m_{\mathrm{time}}}(\tau))\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau) = \sum_{s\in\mathcal S}d_b(s)F(s).\] This follows by induction on \(m_{\mathrm{time}}\). At the initial epoch the identity is Assumption 3. For the induction step, Assumption 2 and Assumption 1 give the behavior peeling identity \[\int F(S_{m_{\mathrm{time}}+1}(\tau))\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau) = \int \sum_{x\in\mathcal S}P_b(S_{m_{\mathrm{time}}}(\tau),x)F(x)\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau).\] Using the induction hypothesis with the bounded function \(s\mapsto \sum_x P_b(s,x)F(x)\), the right-hand side becomes \[\sum_{s\in\mathcal S}d_b(s)\sum_{x\in\mathcal S}P_b(s,x)F(x) = \sum_{x\in\mathcal S}\left(\sum_{s\in\mathcal S}d_b(s)P_b(s,x)\right)F(x) = \sum_{x\in\mathcal S}d_b(x)F(x),\] where the last equality is the stationarity equation for the behavior stationary law \(d_b\), part of the model-class data in Definition 10. Taking the zero-support indicator \[\chi_{\mathrm{null}}(s):=\mathbf 1\{d_b(s)=0\}\] at \(m_{\mathrm{time}}=t+1\) gives \[\int \mathbf 1\{d_b(S_{t+1}(\tau))=0\}\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau)=0,\] so \(d_b(S_{t+1}(\tau))>0\) for \(\mathbb P_M\)-almost every \(\tau\). Hence \[\Phi_{\gamma,k}(S_{t+1}(\tau)) = \widetilde\Phi_{\gamma,k}(S_{t+1}(\tau)) \quad\text{for }\mathbb P_M\text{-almost every }\tau .\]

  6. Put \[A(\tau):=Z_t(k)(O(\tau)), \qquad \overline\Phi:= \int \widetilde\Phi_{\gamma,k}(S_{t+1}(\tau))\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau).\] Using the almost-sure replacement from the previous step, both \[\int A(\tau)\Phi_{\gamma,k}(S_{t+1}(\tau))\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau) = \int A(\tau)\widetilde\Phi_{\gamma,k}(S_{t+1}(\tau))\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau)\] and \[\int \Phi_{\gamma,k}(S_{t+1}(\tau))\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau) = \int \widetilde\Phi_{\gamma,k}(S_{t+1}(\tau))\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau) = \overline\Phi\] hold; the integrability needed for these replacements follows from the finite state space and the score bound above. The centered representation from the first step therefore gives \[\left|\operatorname{Cov}_{\mathrm{obs}}(Z_t(k),Z_u(k))\right| = \left| \int A(\tau)\bigl(\widetilde\Phi_{\gamma,k}(S_{t+1}(\tau))-\overline\Phi\bigr) \,d\mathbb P_M(\tau) \right|.\] The oscillation bound in the fourth step implies, for every \(\tau\), \[\left|\widetilde\Phi_{\gamma,k}(S_{t+1}(\tau))-\overline\Phi\right| \le 2\alpha^\gamma,\] because \(\overline\Phi\) is an average of values of \(\widetilde\Phi_{\gamma,k}\). Therefore, using the unit \(L^1\) bound from the second step, \[\left|\operatorname{Cov}_{\mathrm{obs}}(Z_t(k),Z_u(k))\right| \le \int |A(\tau)|\,2\alpha^\gamma\,d\mathbb P_M(\tau) \le 2\alpha^\gamma = 2\alpha^{h-k-1}.\]

∎

Together, Lemmas 8, 9, 10, 11, and 12 decompose the sampling fluctuation into a weighted-window term and a geometric dependence tail. The overlap condition controls the inflation inside the window, while contraction controls pairs whose windows are separated by at least one time step.

The next statement sums those covariance bounds over future times. It is the finite-sample accounting step that turns the local bounds into a variance inequality for the average score.

Lemma 13 [lem:phiw-future-covariance-sum] (Aggregate future-covariance bound).

Let \(T\ge1\), \(k\in\mathbb N\), \(t_0,\zeta,C\in\mathbb R\), and let \(M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)\) be as in Definition 10. Write \(L=e^{\zeta}\), \(\alpha=e^{-1/t_0}\), and \(\mathscr T_k=\{t:k\le t\le T-1\}\). Then, for every \(t\in\mathscr T_k\), the depth-\(k\) scores of Definition 16 satisfy, under the observed trajectory law of \(M\), \[\sum_{\substack{u\in\mathscr T_k\\ t<u}} \bigl|\operatorname{Cov}_{\mathrm{obs}}\bigl(Z_t(k),Z_u(k)\bigr)\bigr| \le \frac{2L^{k+1}}{L-1}+\frac{2}{1-\alpha}.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 13.

Fix \(t\in\mathscr T_k\). By Definition 10, \(0<\zeta\) and \(0<t_0\), so \[L=e^\zeta>1, \qquad 0\le \alpha=e^{-1/t_0}<1 .\] The same membership supplies the model restrictions used by the covariance estimates below.

  1. Define the future index set and its two lag classes by \[\mathscr U_t:=\{u\in\mathscr T_k:t<u\}, \qquad \lambda_{t,u}:=u-t\quad (u\in\mathscr U_t),\] and \[\mathscr N_t:=\{u\in\mathscr U_t:\lambda_{t,u}\le k\}, \qquad \mathscr G_t:=\{u\in\mathscr U_t:k+1\le \lambda_{t,u}\}.\] For each \(u\in\mathscr U_t\), the lag \(\lambda_{t,u}\) is a positive integer, hence exactly one of \(\lambda_{t,u}\le k\) and \(k+1\le\lambda_{t,u}\) holds. Thus \[\mathscr U_t=\mathscr N_t\,\dot\cup\,\mathscr G_t .\] Writing \[\Gamma_t(u):= \bigl|\operatorname{Cov}_{\mathrm{obs}}\bigl(Z_t(k),Z_u(k)\bigr)\bigr|, \qquad u\in\mathscr U_t,\] we get \[\sum_{u\in\mathscr U_t}\Gamma_t(u) = \sum_{u\in\mathscr N_t}\Gamma_t(u) + \sum_{u\in\mathscr G_t}\Gamma_t(u).\]

  2. For \(u\in\mathscr N_t\), the lag identity is \(u=t+\lambda_{t,u}\) and \(1\le\lambda_{t,u}\le k\). Since \(k\le t\) and \(L\ge1\), Lemma 9 gives \[\Gamma_t(u)\le 2L^{k+1-\lambda_{t,u}}.\] Therefore \[\sum_{u\in\mathscr N_t}\Gamma_t(u) \le 2L\sum_{u\in\mathscr N_t}L^{k-\lambda_{t,u}}.\] The map \[j_{\mathrm{near}}(u):=k-\lambda_{t,u}, \qquad u\in\mathscr N_t,\] is injective and takes its values in \(\{0,\ldots,k-1\}\). Since \(L\ge0\), \[\sum_{u\in\mathscr N_t}L^{k-\lambda_{t,u}} \le \sum_{0\le j<k}L^j .\] For \(L>1\), \[\sum_{0\le j<k}L^j = \frac{L^k-1}{L-1} \le \frac{L^k}{L-1}.\] Combining the last three displays yields \[\sum_{u\in\mathscr N_t}\Gamma_t(u) \le \frac{2L^{k+1}}{L-1}.\]

  3. For \(u\in\mathscr G_t\), the lag identity is \(u=t+\lambda_{t,u}\) and \(k+1\le\lambda_{t,u}\). Hence Lemma 12 gives \[\Gamma_t(u) \le 2\alpha^{\lambda_{t,u}-k-1}.\] Thus \[\sum_{u\in\mathscr G_t}\Gamma_t(u) \le 2\sum_{u\in\mathscr G_t}\alpha^{\lambda_{t,u}-k-1}.\] The map \[j_{\mathrm{sep}}(u):=\lambda_{t,u}-k-1, \qquad u\in\mathscr G_t,\] is injective and takes its values in \(\{0,\ldots,T-1\}\). Since \(\alpha\ge0\), \[\sum_{u\in\mathscr G_t}\alpha^{\lambda_{t,u}-k-1} \le \sum_{0\le j<T}\alpha^j .\] Because \(0\le\alpha<1\), \[\sum_{0\le j<T}\alpha^j \le \sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha^j = \frac{1}{1-\alpha}.\] Consequently, \[\sum_{u\in\mathscr G_t}\Gamma_t(u) \le \frac{2}{1-\alpha}.\]

  4. Adding the two bounds over the disjoint decomposition of \(\mathscr U_t\) gives \[\sum_{\substack{u\in\mathscr T_k\\ t<u}} \bigl|\operatorname{Cov}_{\mathrm{obs}}\bigl(Z_t(k),Z_u(k)\bigr)\bigr| = \sum_{u\in\mathscr U_t}\Gamma_t(u) \le \frac{2L^{k+1}}{L-1}+\frac{2}{1-\alpha}.\]

∎

Lemma 13 supplies the two terms that reappear in the assembled variance bound. The geometric sum over overlapping lags gives the policy-overlap contribution, and the contraction tail gives the dependence contribution.

Lemma 14 [lem:phiw-raw-variance] (Raw variance assembly).

Let \(T\) and \(k\) be nonnegative integers, let \(t_0,\zeta,C\in\mathbb R\), and let \(M\) be a raw POMDP experiment. Write \(L=e^\zeta\) for the policy-overlap factor and \(\alpha=e^{-1/t_0}\) for the contraction factor. Suppose that

  • (Horizon.) \(T\ge 1\).

  • (Model class.) \(M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)\), with \(\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)\) as in Definition 10.

  • (History depth.) \(k\le \lfloor T/2\rfloor\).

For the depth-\(k\) scores \(Z_t(k)\) of Definition 16, form the unclipped partial-history average \[\frac{1}{T-k}\sum_{t=k}^{T-1} Z_t(k).\] Under the observed trajectory law obtained by projecting the full trajectory law of \(M\) onto observed histories, its variance satisfies \[\operatorname{Var}\!\left(\frac{1}{T-k}\sum_{t=k}^{T-1} Z_t(k)\right) \le \frac{2}{T}\left\{ L^{k+1}\left(1+\frac{4}{L-1}\right) + \frac{4}{1-\alpha} \right\}.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 14.

Let \(\mathbb P_{\mathrm{obs}}\) denote the observed trajectory law and let \(\mathbb E_{\mathrm{obs}}\) denote expectation under this law. Let \(\mathbb P_{\mathrm{full}}\) denote the full trajectory law carried by \(M\), and let \(\mathbb E_{\mathrm{full}}\) denote expectation under that law. Put \[\mathscr T_k:=\{t\in\{0,\ldots,T-1\}: k\le t\}, \qquad \mathfrak B_k:= L^{k+1}\left(1+\frac{4}{L-1}\right)+\frac{4}{1-\alpha}.\] For \(t\in\mathscr T_k\), write \[\Gamma_t^{(k)} := \prod_{\substack{0\le j\le T-1\\ j\le t,\ t-j\le k}}\rho_j, \qquad Z_t(k)=Y_t\Gamma_t^{(k)} .\] Since \(M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)\), Definition 10 gives \(t_0>0\), \(\zeta>0\), hence \[L=e^\zeta>1, \qquad 0\le \alpha=e^{-1/t_0}<1.\] Also \(k\le \lfloor T/2\rfloor\) implies \[|\mathscr T_k|=T-k>0, \qquad \frac{T}{2}\le T-k.\] In particular \(\mathfrak B_k\ge0\). The raw average is \[\frac{1}{T-k}\sum_{t=k}^{T-1} Z_t(k) = \frac{1}{T-k}\sum_{t\in\mathscr T_k}Z_t(k).\]

  1. For each \(t\in\mathscr T_k\), Lemma 8 applies with the sequential-ignorability, policy-overlap, kernel-law, and bounded-reward clauses supplied by Definition 10, together with \(L=e^\zeta\ge 1\) and \(k\le t\). It gives \[\mathbb E_{\mathrm{obs}}\bigl[Z_t(k)^2\bigr] \le L^{k+1}.\] Using the standard identity \[\operatorname{Var}_{\mathrm{obs}}(Z_t(k)) = \mathbb E_{\mathrm{obs}}\bigl[Z_t(k)^2\bigr] -\bigl(\mathbb E_{\mathrm{obs}}Z_t(k)\bigr)^2,\] we obtain \[\operatorname{Var}_{\mathrm{obs}}(Z_t(k)) \le L^{k+1}.\]

  2. Fix \(t\in\mathscr T_k\). The aggregate covariance estimate in Lemma 13 applies to the same model class and the same future index set \(\mathscr T_k\). Therefore \[\sum_{\substack{u\in\mathscr T_k\\ t<u}} \left|\operatorname{Cov}_{\mathrm{obs}}\bigl(Z_t(k),Z_u(k)\bigr)\right| \le \frac{2L^{k+1}}{L-1}+\frac{2}{1-\alpha}.\]

  3. Now expand the variance of the finite sum: \[\operatorname{Var}_{\mathrm{obs}}\!\left(\sum_{t\in\mathscr T_k}Z_t(k)\right) = \sum_{t\in\mathscr T_k}\sum_{u\in\mathscr T_k} \operatorname{Cov}_{\mathrm{obs}}\bigl(Z_t(k),Z_u(k)\bigr).\] Bounding each covariance by its absolute value and then using covariance symmetry gives \[\operatorname{Var}_{\mathrm{obs}}\!\left(\sum_{t\in\mathscr T_k}Z_t(k)\right) \le \sum_{t\in\mathscr T_k}\operatorname{Var}_{\mathrm{obs}}(Z_t(k)) + 2\sum_{t\in\mathscr T_k} \sum_{\substack{u\in\mathscr T_k\\ t<u}} \left|\operatorname{Cov}_{\mathrm{obs}}\bigl(Z_t(k),Z_u(k)\bigr)\right|.\] Using the diagonal and future-sum bounds, \[\operatorname{Var}_{\mathrm{obs}}\!\left(\sum_{t\in\mathscr T_k}Z_t(k)\right) \le |\mathscr T_k|L^{k+1} + 2|\mathscr T_k| \left( \frac{2L^{k+1}}{L-1} + \frac{2}{1-\alpha} \right) = (T-k)\mathfrak B_k.\]

  4. Finally, \[\operatorname{Var}_{\mathrm{obs}}\!\left( \frac{1}{T-k}\sum_{t\in\mathscr T_k}Z_t(k) \right) = \frac{1}{(T-k)^2} \operatorname{Var}_{\mathrm{obs}}\!\left(\sum_{t\in\mathscr T_k}Z_t(k)\right) \le \frac{\mathfrak B_k}{T-k}.\] Since \(T/2\le T-k\), \(T>0\), and \(\mathfrak B_k\ge0\), \[\frac{\mathfrak B_k}{T-k} \le \frac{2\mathfrak B_k}{T}.\] Substituting the definition of \(\mathfrak B_k\) gives \[\operatorname{Var}_{\mathrm{obs}}\!\left(\frac{1}{T-k}\sum_{t=k}^{T-1} Z_t(k)\right) \le \frac{2}{T}\left\{ L^{k+1}\left(1+\frac{4}{L-1}\right) + \frac{4}{1-\alpha} \right\}.\]

∎

The two terms in Lemma 14 have distinct origins: multiplying observable policy ratios over a window produces the \(L^{k+1}\) term, while serial dependence contributes the contraction term. The bound is stated for the unclipped average because the displayed variance is the component used before the clipped estimator’s squared-error risk is assembled.

The next statement supplies the fixed-radius upper bound for the partial-history importance-weighted estimator. It gives both the rate bound for the balanced depth and the corresponding variance calculation for the unclipped average.

Lemma 15 [lem:phiw-upper] (Balanced PHIW upper bound).

Fix constants satisfying

  • (Mixing scale.) \(t_0>0\).

  • (Policy-overlap scale.) \(\zeta>0\).

  • (Latent-overlap radius.) \(C\ge 1\).

Let \(k_T\) be the balanced history depth in Definition 17, and set \[\beta=\frac{2}{2+t_0\zeta}.\] Then there exist constants \(c^\star>0\) and \(T_\star\in\mathbb N\) such that, for every \(T\ge T_\star\), the estimator-specific worst-case squared risk from Definition 13 of the observable PHIW estimator \(\widehat\theta_{k_T}\) from Definition 16 satisfies \[R_T(t_0,\zeta,C;\widehat\theta_{k_T}) \le c^\star T^{-\beta}.\] Moreover, for every \(T\ge T_\star\), every finite alphabet sizes \(n_X,n_H\), and every raw POMDP experiment \(M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)\), the variance under the observed trajectory law induced by \(M\) of the unclipped partial-history average \[\frac{1}{T-k_T}\sum_{t=k_T}^{T-1} Z_t(k_T)\] satisfies \[\operatorname{Var} \!\left(\frac{1}{T-k_T}\sum_{t=k_T}^{T-1} Z_t(k_T)\right) \le \frac{2}{T} \left[ e^{\zeta(k_T+1)} \left(1+\frac{4}{e^\zeta-1}\right) + \frac{4}{1-e^{-1/t_0}} \right].\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 15.

  1. Define \[\alpha=\exp(-1/t_0),\qquad L=\exp(\zeta),\qquad \beta=\frac{2}{2+t_0\zeta}, \qquad q_C=\frac{C-1}{C}.\] The assumptions \(t_0>0\) and \(\zeta>0\) give \[0<\alpha<1,\qquad 1<L,\qquad 0<\beta<1.\] Also \(C\ge1\) implies \[0\le q_C\le1,\qquad q_C^2\le1.\] Let \[\mathfrak d = 2\log(1/\alpha)+\log L = \frac{2}{t_0}+\zeta .\] Then \(\mathfrak d>0\) and \[\beta=\frac{2/t_0}{\mathfrak d}.\] Since \(\log T=o(T)\), there is an integer \(T_{\mathrm{bal}}\ge1\) such that, for every \(T\ge T_{\mathrm{bal}}\), \[0\le \frac{\log T}{\mathfrak d}\le \frac{T}{2}.\] For such \(T\), Definition 17 gives \[k_T=\left\lfloor\frac{\log T}{\mathfrak d}\right\rfloor ,\] and hence \[\frac{\log T}{\mathfrak d}-1<k_T\le \frac{\log T}{\mathfrak d}.\] Therefore \[\alpha^{2k_T} = \exp(-2k_T/t_0) \le \exp(2/t_0)\,T^{-\beta},\] and \[\frac{L^{k_T+1}}{T} = \exp\{(k_T+1)\zeta-\log T\} \le \exp(\zeta)\,T^{-\beta}.\] With \[c_{\mathrm{bal}}:=\exp(2/t_0)+\exp(\zeta)>0,\] we have, for every \(T\ge T_{\mathrm{bal}}\), \[\alpha^{2k_T}+\frac{L^{k_T+1}}{T} \le c_{\mathrm{bal}}T^{-\beta}.\]

  2. Set \[\mathsf A:=1+\frac{4}{L-1}, \qquad \mathsf B:=\frac{4}{1-\alpha}, \qquad \mathsf D:=\max\{4,2\mathsf A\},\] and define \[c^\star:=\mathsf D\,c_{\mathrm{bal}}+2\mathsf B, \qquad T_\star:=T_{\mathrm{bal}}.\] Since \(L>1\) and \(\alpha<1\), the constants \(\mathsf A,\mathsf B,\mathsf D\) are positive, and so \(c^\star>0\).

  3. Fix \(T\ge T_\star\). Then \(1\le T\), \(k_T\le T/2\), and \(k_T<T\). For every \(M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)\), Lemma 34, applied with \(k=k_T\), gives \[\mathbb E_M\!\left[ \{\widehat\theta_{k_T}-\theta(K,e)\}^2 \right] \le 4q_C^2\alpha^{2k_T} + \frac{2}{T}\left(L^{k_T+1}\mathsf A+\mathsf B\right).\] Using \(q_C^2\le1\), \(\mathsf D\ge4\), and \(\mathsf D\ge2\mathsf A\), \[4q_C^2\alpha^{2k_T} + \frac{2}{T}\left(L^{k_T+1}\mathsf A+\mathsf B\right) \le \mathsf D\left(\alpha^{2k_T}+\frac{L^{k_T+1}}{T}\right) + 2\mathsf B\,T^{-1}.\] The balance bound from the first step and the inequality \(T^{-1}\le T^{-\beta}\), valid because \(T\ge1\) and \(0<\beta<1\), yield \[\mathbb E_M\!\left[ \{\widehat\theta_{k_T}-\theta(K,e)\}^2 \right] \le \mathsf D c_{\mathrm{bal}}T^{-\beta} + 2\mathsf B T^{-\beta} = c^\star T^{-\beta}.\] There are two cases. If the indexed class \(\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)\) is nonempty, the pointwise bound over every member of the class, together with the estimator-specific risk definition in Definition 13, gives \[R_T(t_0,\zeta,C;\widehat\theta_{k_T}) \le c^\star T^{-\beta}.\] If the indexed class is empty, the real-valued worst-case-risk functional used for \(R_T\) has empty supremum equal to \(0\). Since \(c^\star>0\), \(T\ge1\), and \(T^{-\beta}\ge0\), the bound also holds in this case.

  4. It remains to record the variance assertion. For every \(T\ge T_\star\), the definition of \(T_\star\) gives \(1\le T\), and the minimum in Definition 17 gives \(k_T\le\lfloor T/2\rfloor\). Apply Lemma 14 with \(k=k_T\), \(L=e^\zeta\), and \(\alpha=e^{-1/t_0}\). Its conclusion is exactly \[\operatorname{Var}_M\!\left( \frac1{T-k_T}\sum_{t=k_T}^{T-1}Z_t(k_T) \right) \le \frac{2}{T}\left[ e^{\zeta(k_T+1)}\left(1+\frac{4}{e^\zeta-1}\right) +\frac{4}{1-e^{-1/t_0}} \right],\] uniformly over the finite alphabet sizes and every \(M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)\), as required.

∎

The bound in Lemma 15 identifies the observable estimator used for the upper side of the fixed-\(C\) comparison. The variance display isolates the cost of multiplying observable policy ratios over a window of length \(k_T\) and the serial-dependence contribution controlled by contraction. Balancing those terms gives the exponent \(\beta\) under fixed overlap and fixed latent-stationary domination.

For the lower side, the appendix uses the signed-depth alternatives introduced in Definition 18. The next two statements record the contraction and stationary-law calculations that make the construction a member of the same finite-POMDP class.

Lemma 16 [lem:signed-depth-constant-policy-contraction] (Contraction of the constant-policy signed-depth kernel).

Let \(t_0>0\), \(\zeta>0\), \(C\ge1\), \(Q\ge1\), \(v\in\{-1,+1\}\) and \(r\in[0,1]\), and write \(\alpha=e^{-1/t_0}\). Let \(P^{(r)}\) be the transition kernel induced on the joint state space \(\mathcal S_Q=\{0\}\times(\{0,\ldots,Q\}\times\{-1,+1\})\) of the signed-depth alternative of Definition 18 with terminal depth \(Q\) and index \(v\) by the constant policy \(a_r\) that plays action one with probability \(r\). Then, for all probability vectors \(\mu,\mu'\) on \(\mathcal S_Q\), \[\|\mu P^{(r)}-\mu'P^{(r)}\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \le \alpha\|\mu-\mu'\|_{\mathrm{TV}} .\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 16.

Let \[\varepsilon_C=\min\{(C-1)/2,1/2\},\qquad \alpha=\exp(-1/t_0).\] For \(\sigma=(0,j,u)\) and \(\sigma'=(0,j',u')\) in \(\mathcal S_Q\), define the refresh law \[\eta_{C,Q}(\sigma') = \mathbf 1\{j'=0\}\,\frac{1+u'\varepsilon_C}{2},\] and define the residual kernel \[\Gamma_{C,r,Q}(\sigma,\sigma') = \begin{cases} \eta_{C,Q}(\sigma'), & j=Q,\\[2mm] \dfrac{1-r}{2}+r\,\mathbf 1\{u'=u\}, & j<Q \text{ and } j'=j+1,\\[2mm] 0, & \text{otherwise.} \end{cases}\] Expanding the transition rule in Definition 18 under the constant policy \(a_r(1)=r\), \(a_r(0)=1-r\), gives, for every \(\sigma,\sigma'\in\mathcal S_Q\), \[P^{(r)}(\sigma,\sigma') = (1-\alpha)\eta_{C,Q}(\sigma') + \alpha\,\Gamma_{C,r,Q}(\sigma,\sigma').\] Indeed, at terminal depth \(j=Q\) both sides equal \(\eta_{C,Q}(\sigma')\); at depths \(j<Q\), the reset part contributes \((1-\alpha)\eta_{C,Q}(\sigma')\), while the advance-to-\(j+1\) part contributes \(\alpha[(1-r)/2+r\mathbf 1\{u'=u\}]\).

The residual kernel is stochastic. Since \(C\ge1\), one has \(\varepsilon_C\in[0,1/2]\), so the two reset weights are nonnegative and \[\sum_{u'\in\{-1,+1\}}\frac{1+u'\varepsilon_C}{2}=1.\] If \(j=Q\), summing \(\Gamma_{C,r,Q}(\sigma,\cdot)\) over \(\mathcal S_Q\) therefore gives \(1\). If \(j<Q\), the only nonzero entries have \(j'=j+1\), and their two sign weights sum to \[\left(\frac{1-r}{2}+r\right)+\frac{1-r}{2}=1,\] with nonnegativity following from \(r\in[0,1]\). Thus \[\Gamma_{C,r,Q}(\sigma,\sigma')\ge0 \quad\text{and}\quad \sum_{\sigma'\in\mathcal S_Q}\Gamma_{C,r,Q}(\sigma,\sigma')=1\] for every \(\sigma\in\mathcal S_Q\).

For any signed vector \(\lambda\) on \(\mathcal S_Q\), stochasticity of \(\Gamma_{C,r,Q}\) gives the total-variation nonexpansiveness bound \[\begin{aligned} \|\lambda\Gamma_{C,r,Q}\|_{\mathrm{TV}} &= \frac12\sum_{\sigma'}\left| \sum_{\sigma}\lambda(\sigma)\Gamma_{C,r,Q}(\sigma,\sigma') \right| \\ &\le \frac12\sum_{\sigma'}\sum_{\sigma} |\lambda(\sigma)|\Gamma_{C,r,Q}(\sigma,\sigma') \\ &= \frac12\sum_{\sigma}|\lambda(\sigma)| \sum_{\sigma'}\Gamma_{C,r,Q}(\sigma,\sigma') = \|\lambda\|_{\mathrm{TV}} . \end{aligned}\]

Now take probability vectors \(\mu,\mu'\). Because \(\sum_\sigma\mu(\sigma)=\sum_\sigma\mu'(\sigma)=1\), the common refresh term cancels: \[\begin{aligned} (\mu P^{(r)}-\mu'P^{(r)})(\sigma') &= \alpha\sum_{\sigma} (\mu(\sigma)-\mu'(\sigma))\Gamma_{C,r,Q}(\sigma,\sigma') . \end{aligned}\] Since \(\alpha=\exp(-1/t_0)>0\), the preceding display and nonexpansiveness yield \[\|\mu P^{(r)}-\mu'P^{(r)}\|_{\mathrm{TV}} = \alpha\|(\mu-\mu')\Gamma_{C,r,Q}\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \le \alpha\|\mu-\mu'\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.\] This is the claimed contraction.

∎
Lemma 17 [lem:signed-depth-stationary-law] (Closed form of the signed-depth stationary law).

Let \(t_0>0\), \(\zeta>0\), \(C\ge1\), \(Q\ge1\), \(v\in\{-1,+1\}\) and \(r\in[0,1]\). Write \(\alpha=e^{-1/t_0}\), \(\varepsilon_C=\min\{(C-1)/2,1/2\}\), and \(w_j=(1-\alpha)\alpha^{j}/(1-\alpha^{Q+1})\). Let \(P^{(r)}\) be the kernel induced on \(\mathcal S_Q\) by the constant policy \(a_r\) for the signed-depth alternative of Definition 18 with terminal depth \(Q\) and index \(v\), and let \(d_r\) be the stationary law selected for \(P^{(r)}\). Then, for every hidden state \(h=(j(h),u(h))\), \[d_r(0,h) = w_{j(h)}\,\frac{1+u(h)\varepsilon_Cr^{j(h)}}{2}.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 17.

Write \[\bar d_r(0,(j,u)) := w_j\,\frac{1+u\varepsilon_C r^j}{2}, \qquad j\in\{0,\ldots,Q\},\quad u\in\{-1,+1\}.\] We prove that this probability vector is stationary for \(P^{(r)}\), and then use the contraction statement to identify it with the selected stationary law.

  1. Since \(t_0>0\), the value \(\alpha=e^{-1/t_0}\) lies in \((0,1)\). Also \(C\ge 1\) gives \(0\le \varepsilon_C\le 1/2\), and \(r\in[0,1]\) gives \(0\le r^j\le 1\). Hence every factor \((1+u\varepsilon_C r^j)/2\) is nonnegative. For each fixed depth \(j\), \[\sum_{u\in\{-1,+1\}} w_j\,\frac{1+u\varepsilon_C r^j}{2} =w_j,\] and the geometric normalization gives \[\sum_{j=0}^Q w_j = \frac{1-\alpha}{1-\alpha^{Q+1}}\sum_{j=0}^Q \alpha^j =1.\] Thus \(\bar d_r\) is a probability vector on \(\mathcal S_Q\).

  2. The state kernel induced by the constant policy \(a_r\) has the following hidden-state transition probabilities, obtained from the signed-depth dynamics in Definition 18 by averaging the action-one and action-zero advance weights. If \(j<Q\), then \[P^{(r)}\bigl((0,(j,u)),(0,(0,u'))\bigr) =(1-\alpha)\frac{1+u'\varepsilon_C}{2},\] and \[P^{(r)}\bigl((0,(j,u)),(0,(j+1,u'))\bigr) = \alpha\,\frac{1+uu'r}{2}.\] If \(j=Q\), then \[P^{(r)}\bigl((0,(Q,u)),(0,(0,u'))\bigr) = \frac{1+u'\varepsilon_C}{2}.\] The remaining transitions have weight zero.

    For a target state of depth \(0\), summing the displayed reset probabilities gives \[(\bar d_rP^{(r)})(0,(0,u')) = \frac{1+u'\varepsilon_C}{2} \left\{ w_Q+(1-\alpha)\sum_{j=0}^{Q-1}w_j \right\}.\] Since \(\sum_{j=0}^Q w_j=1\), \[w_Q+(1-\alpha)\sum_{j=0}^{Q-1}w_j = (1-\alpha)+\alpha w_Q = (1-\alpha)+ \frac{(1-\alpha)\alpha^{Q+1}}{1-\alpha^{Q+1}} = \frac{1-\alpha}{1-\alpha^{Q+1}} =w_0.\] Therefore \[(\bar d_rP^{(r)})(0,(0,u')) = w_0\,\frac{1+u'\varepsilon_C}{2} = \bar d_r(0,(0,u')).\]

    For a target state of depth \(m\in\{1,\ldots,Q\}\), only depth \(m-1\) can advance to it, so \[(\bar d_rP^{(r)})(0,(m,u')) = \alpha w_{m-1} \sum_{u\in\{-1,+1\}} \frac{1+u\varepsilon_C r^{m-1}}{2} \frac{1+uu'r}{2}.\] The sign sum is \[\sum_{u\in\{-1,+1\}} \frac{1+u\varepsilon_C r^{m-1}}{2} \frac{1+uu'r}{2} = \frac{1+u'\varepsilon_C r^m}{2}.\] Using \(w_m=\alpha w_{m-1}\), this yields \[(\bar d_rP^{(r)})(0,(m,u')) = w_m\,\frac{1+u'\varepsilon_C r^m}{2} = \bar d_r(0,(m,u')).\] Thus \(\bar d_rP^{(r)}=\bar d_r\).

  3. The contraction supplied by Lemma 16 implies uniqueness of stationary probability vectors. Indeed, if \(\lambda\) and \(\lambda'\) are stationary for \(P^{(r)}\), then \[\|\lambda-\lambda'\|_{\mathrm{TV}} = \|\lambda P^{(r)}-\lambda'P^{(r)}\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \le \alpha\|\lambda-\lambda'\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.\] Because \(\alpha<1\) and total variation is nonnegative, the norm is zero, hence \(\lambda=\lambda'\) on the finite state space. The selected law \(d_r\) is stationary because the stationary vector \(\bar d_r\) exists, so uniqueness gives \(d_r=\bar d_r\).

  4. Evaluating the identity \(d_r=\bar d_r\) at the hidden state \(h=(j(h),u(h))\) gives \[d_r(0,h) = w_{j(h)}\,\frac{1+u(h)\varepsilon_C r^{j(h)}}{2},\] as claimed.

∎

Lemma 16 gives the uniform total-variation contraction of the signed-depth transition under any constant action-one probability. Lemma 17 then gives the stationary law explicitly: the depth mass is a truncated geometric sequence, and the sign imbalance is scaled by the action-one probability to the current depth.

Lemma 18 [lem:signed-depth-membership] (Signed-depth membership).

For every \(T\ge 1\), \(Q\ge 1\), \(t_0>0\), \(\zeta>0\), \(C>1\), and \(v\in\{-1,+1\}\), let \(M_{Q,v}^T\) be the signed-depth experiment of Definition 18. Put \[\varepsilon_C=\min\{(C-1)/2,1/2\}, \qquad L=\exp(\zeta), \qquad w_j=\frac{(1-\alpha(t_0))\alpha(t_0)^j}{1-\alpha(t_0)^{Q+1}},\] where \(\alpha(t_0)\) is the mixing factor and \(j=0,\ldots,Q\). Then:

  • (Membership.) \(M_{Q,v}^T\in \mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)\) in the sense of Definition 10.

  • (Behavior stationary law.) For every hidden state \(h\), with decoded depth \(j(h)\in\{0,\ldots,Q\}\) and sign \(u(h)\in\{-1,+1\}\), \[d_b(0,h) = w_{j(h)} \frac{1+u(h)\varepsilon_C L^{-j(h)}}{2}.\]

  • (Constant action-one stationary laws.) For every \(r\in[0,1]\), if \(d_r\) denotes the stationary law induced by the constant policy that chooses action one with probability \(r\), then for every hidden state \(h\), \[d_r(0,h) = w_{j(h)} \frac{1+u(h)\varepsilon_C r^{j(h)}}{2}.\]

  • (Target-over-behavior domination.) For every joint state \(s\), \[d_e(s)\le (1+\varepsilon_C)d_b(s), \qquad 1+\varepsilon_C\le C.\]

The stationary target value of Definition 12 is \[\theta(K,e) = v\,c_0(t_0)\,\varepsilon_C\,w_Q .\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 18.

Write \(\alpha=\alpha(t_0)=\exp(-1/t_0)\), \(L=\exp(\zeta)\), and \(\varepsilon=\varepsilon_C\). Since \(t_0>0\) and \(\zeta>0\), we have \(0<\alpha<1\) and \(1<L\). Also \(C>1\) gives \(C\ge 1\), so \[0\le \varepsilon=\min\{(C-1)/2,1/2\}\le \frac12 .\] For a hidden state \(h\), abbreviate \(j=j(h)\) and \(u=u(h)\in\{-1,+1\}\).

  1. For \(r\in[0,1]\), let \(a_r\) be the constant policy with \(\Pr(A_t=1)=r\), and let \(P^{(r)}\) be the induced transition kernel on the joint state space \[\mathcal S_Q=\{(0,h):h\in\{0,\ldots,Q\}\times\{-1,+1\}\}.\] By Lemma 16, for all probability laws \(\mu\) and \(\mu'\) on \(\mathcal S_Q\), \[\|\mu P^{(r)}-\mu'P^{(r)}\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \le \alpha\|\mu-\mu'\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.\] The behavior policy in Definition 18 is the case \(r=L^{-1}\), while the target policy is the case \(r=1\). The displayed inequality therefore gives the contraction required in Assumption 6 for both policies.

  2. For \(r\in[0,1]\), Lemma 17 identifies the selected stationary law of the constant-policy kernel \(P^{(r)}\): for every hidden state \(h\), \[d_r(0,h) = w_{j(h)} \frac{1+u(h)\varepsilon r^{j(h)}}2 .\] Taking \(r=L^{-1}\) gives the behavior stationary law because the behavior policy in Definition 18 chooses action one with probability \(1/L\), and \((L^{-1})^{j(h)}=L^{-j(h)}\). Thus \[d_b(0,h) = w_{j(h)} \frac{1+u(h)\varepsilon L^{-j(h)}}2 .\] Taking \(r=1\) gives the target stationary law \[d_e(0,h) = w_{j(h)} \frac{1+u(h)\varepsilon}{2}.\]

  3. We now verify the model-class predicates in Definition 10. The embedded experiment is generated by the initial law, the behavior policy, and the time-homogeneous reward-transition kernel in Definition 18. Therefore, for each epoch \(t=0,\ldots,T-1\), \[\mathcal L(Y_t,X_{t+1},H_{t+1}\mid X_{0:t},H_{0:t},A_{0:t},Y_{0:t-1}) = K(\cdot\mid X_t,H_t,A_t),\] which is Assumption 1. The behavior action law is \[b(1\mid0)=L^{-1}, \qquad b(0\mid0)=1-L^{-1}, \qquad b(0\mid0)+b(1\mid0)=1,\] and these two masses are nonnegative because \(L>1\). Since the observed state is the singleton \(\{0\}\), the chronological law factors at every epoch \(t=0,\ldots,T-1\) as \[\begin{aligned} &\mathbb P\!\left\{ \bigl((X_s,H_s)_{s<t},(A_s,Y_s)_{s<t},(X_t,H_t),A_t\bigr) \in d\mathsf h\,da \right\} \\ &\qquad= \mathbb P\!\left\{ \bigl((X_s,H_s)_{s<t},(A_s,Y_s)_{s<t},(X_t,H_t)\bigr) \in d\mathsf h \right\}\,b(da\mid X_t(\mathsf h)), \end{aligned}\] which is Assumption 2. The finite reward symbols are decoded as \(-1\) and \(+1\), so \[\Pr(|Y_t|\le1)=1,\] giving Assumption 4. The policy-overlap inequality is \[e(1\mid0)=1=L\,b(1\mid0), \qquad e(0\mid0)=0\le L\,b(0\mid0),\] so Assumption 5 holds with factor \(L\). The initial law is \[\Pr\{S_0=(0,h)\} = w_{j(h)} \frac{1+u(h)\varepsilon L^{-j(h)}}2 = d_b(0,h),\] where the last equality is the stationary-law formula from the case \(r=L^{-1}\). Thus Assumption 3 holds.

    For the latent stationary overlap, first identify the two selected stationary laws and the initial law on the same cells. The target and behavior formulas from Step 2 give \[d_e(0,h) = w_j\frac{1+u(h)\varepsilon}{2}, \qquad d_b(0,h) = w_j\frac{1+u(h)\varepsilon L^{-j}}{2}.\] The displayed initialization in Definition 18 gives \[\Pr\{S_0=(0,h)\} = w_j\frac{1+u(h)\varepsilon L^{-j}}{2} = d_b(0,h),\] where the last equality is the behavior stationary-law formula from Step 2. The depth mass is nonnegative: since \[0<\alpha<1, \qquad 1-\alpha^{Q+1}>0,\] we have \[w_j = \frac{(1-\alpha)\alpha^j}{1-\alpha^{Q+1}} \ge0 .\] Here \(L^{-j}=(L^j)^{-1}\). Since \(L>1\), we have \(0<L^{-j}\le1\). For \(u(h)=-1\), \[d_e(0,h) = w_j\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2} \le w_j\frac{1-\varepsilon L^{-j}}2 = d_b(0,h) \le (1+\varepsilon)d_b(0,h),\] where the first inequality uses \(\varepsilon L^{-j}\le\varepsilon\), and the last uses \(\varepsilon\ge0\) and nonnegativity of the behavior cell. For \(u(h)=+1\), \[d_e(0,h) = w_j\frac{1+\varepsilon}{2} \le w_j\frac{1+\varepsilon}{2}(1+\varepsilon L^{-j}) = (1+\varepsilon)d_b(0,h),\] because \(w_j\ge0\) and \(1+\varepsilon\ge0\), so the multiplier \(w_j(1+\varepsilon)/2\) is nonnegative, while \(1+\varepsilon L^{-j}\ge1\). Multiplying this last inequality by that nonnegative multiplier gives the displayed inequality, and its right-hand side is \((1+\varepsilon)d_b(0,h)\) by the formula for \(d_b\). Every joint state has the unique observed coordinate \(0\), so these two sign cases give \[d_e(s)\le (1+\varepsilon)d_b(s) \qquad\text{for every }s\in\mathcal S_Q.\] Moreover \[1+\varepsilon = 1+\min\{(C-1)/2,1/2\} \le C,\] because either \(\varepsilon=(C-1)/2\), giving \(1+\varepsilon=(C+1)/2\le C\), or \(\varepsilon=1/2\), which is possible only when \(C\ge2\). Since \(d_b(s)\ge0\), \[d_e(s)\le (1+\varepsilon)d_b(s)\le C d_b(s),\] so Assumption 7 holds.

    It remains to collect the remaining requirements of Definition 10. The horizon requirement \(1\le T\) is the hypothesis \(T\ge1\) of the present lemma. The observed alphabet of \(M_{Q,v}^T\) is the singleton \(\{0\}\), the latent alphabet is the \(2(Q+1)\)-element set \(\{0,\ldots,Q\}\times\{-1,+1\}\), and the action alphabet is \(\{0,1\}\); all three are finite, and the action alphabet has exactly two elements, as the class requires. The parameter requirements \(0<t_0\) and \(0<\zeta\) are hypotheses of the lemma, and they fix \(L=\exp(\zeta)\) and \(\alpha=\exp(-1/t_0)\) as the overlap and contraction constants used throughout. Together with the seven assumptions verified above – the Markov kernel, sequential ignorability, stationary start, bounded rewards, policy overlap, uniform contraction, and latent stationary overlap – this is every defining property of Definition 10, and therefore \(M_{Q,v}^T\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)\).

  4. It remains to compute the target value. The two-point alternative label enters the reward law only through its numeric sign: the positive label contributes \(+1\), the negative label contributes \(-1\), and in the statement this multiplier is denoted by \(v\in\{-1,+1\}\). Under the target policy the action is always one. From the reward weights in Definition 18, \[g_e(0,h) = \sum_a e(a\mid0)\,E_K[Y_t\mid S_t=(0,h),A_t=a] = v\,c_0(t_0)\,u(h)\,\mathbf 1\{j(h)=Q\}.\] Using Definition 12 and the already computed target stationary law, \[\theta(K,e) = \sum_h w_{j(h)} \frac{1+u(h)\varepsilon}{2}\, v\,c_0(t_0)\,u(h)\,\mathbf 1\{j(h)=Q\}.\] Only depth \(Q\) contributes, and summing over the two signs gives \[\theta(K,e) = v\,c_0(t_0)\,w_Q \left[ \frac{1+\varepsilon}{2} - \frac{1-\varepsilon}{2} \right] = v\,c_0(t_0)\,\varepsilon\,w_Q .\] This is the claimed value formula.

∎

The stationary laws in Lemma 18 show how the lower construction keeps the target-over-behavior ratio bounded while concentrating the signal at terminal depth. The terminal value is proportional to the stationary depth mass \(w_Q\), so increasing \(Q\) makes the two alternatives harder to separate from observed data while preserving the fixed latent-overlap radius.

The likelihood calculation rests on finite-prefix posterior bounds, a rare-action second moment, a bootstrap factor that makes the posterior control uniform in the terminal depth, signed-mass identities at terminal depth, exact conditional reward means, and finite-word KL inequalities. The next group develops these ingredients in the order in which the observed-path comparison uses them.

Lemma 19 [lem:posterior-lower-window-power] (Posterior window power bound).

Let \(N,Q,k,d\in\mathbb N\), let \(t_0,\zeta,C,b\in\mathbb R\), let \(v\in\{-1,+1\}\), and let \(o=((a_0,y_0),\ldots,(a_{k-1},y_{k-1}))\) be an observed prefix for the signed-depth family of Definition 18. Write \[c_0(t_0)=\frac{1-\alpha(t_0)}{4},\] and, for \(0\le m\le k\), write \(o_{\le m}\) for the first \(m\) observed pairs. For any observed prefix \(o'\), define the raw terminal-depth posterior by \[\pi_v(o') = \frac{\sum_h \mathbf 1\{J(h)=Q\}\lambda_v(h;o')} {\sum_h \lambda_v(h;o')}.\] Assume:

  • (Nonnegative amplitude.) \(0\le c_0(t_0)\).

  • (Nonnegative base.) \(0\le 1-c_0(t_0)b\).

  • (Window length.) \(d\le k\).

  • (Strict-prefix posterior bound.) For every \(i\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}\), \[\pi_v(o_{\le i})\le b.\]

Define the final \(d\)-prefix lower window by \[\Lambda_d(o) = \prod_{\substack{0\le i<k\\ k-d\le i}} \left(1-c_0(t_0)\pi_v(o_{\le i})\right).\] Then \[\bigl(1-c_0(t_0)b\bigr)^d\le \Lambda_d(o).\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 19.

We prove the slightly stronger assertion with the prefix length left variable. For a prefix \(o\) of length \(n\), write \[\Lambda_d^{(n)}(o) = \prod_{\substack{0\le i<n\\ n-d\le i}} \left(1-c_0(t_0)\pi_v(o_{\le i})\right),\] with the convention that an empty product is \(1\).

1. If \(d=0\), then the condition \(n\le i<n\) selects no indices, so \[\Lambda_0^{(n)}(o)=1=(1-c_0(t_0)b)^0 .\] This proves the base case.

2. Assume the claim has been proved for \(d\). Consider a prefix \(o\) of length \(n\) with \(d+1\le n\). If \(n=0\), this inequality is impossible, so there is no case to prove. Thus write \(n=k+1\). Let \(o^{-}\) be the length-\(k\) prefix obtained by dropping the last observed pair: \[o^{-}(i)=o(i),\qquad 0\le i<k .\] For every \(0\le i<k\), the first \(i\) observations of \(o^{-}\) are exactly the first \(i\) observations of \(o\): \[(o^{-})_{\le i}=o_{\le i}.\] Hence the strict-prefix posterior hypothesis for \(o\) gives \[\pi_v\bigl((o^{-})_{\le i}\bigr)=\pi_v(o_{\le i})\le b, \qquad 0\le i<k .\] Since \(d+1\le k+1\), we have \(d\le k\), so the induction hypothesis applied to \(o^{-}\) yields \[(1-c_0(t_0)b)^d\le \Lambda_d^{(k)}(o^{-}).\]

3. The final-window product for length \(k+1\) splits into the length-\(k\) final-window product for \(o^{-}\) and the last factor: \[\Lambda_{d+1}^{(k+1)}(o) = \Lambda_d^{(k)}(o^{-}) \left(1-c_0(t_0)\pi_v(o_{\le k})\right).\] Indeed, the threshold satisfies \[(k+1)-(d+1)=k-d,\] so the eligible indices before the last one are precisely the eligible indices in the length-\(k\) window, and the remaining eligible index is \(i=k\).

4. The posterior hypothesis at the last strict prefix gives \(\pi_v(o_{\le k})\le b\). Because \(0\le c_0(t_0)\), multiplying preserves the inequality, \[c_0(t_0)\pi_v(o_{\le k})\le c_0(t_0)b,\] and subtracting from \(1\) reverses the side on which the smaller product appears: \[1-c_0(t_0)b\le 1-c_0(t_0)\pi_v(o_{\le k}).\] Together with the assumed nonnegativity \(0\le 1-c_0(t_0)b\), we have \[0\le (1-c_0(t_0)b)^d .\] Combining this nonnegativity, the induction bound, and the last-factor bound gives \[(1-c_0(t_0)b)^{d+1} = (1-c_0(t_0)b)^d(1-c_0(t_0)b) \le \Lambda_d^{(k)}(o^{-}) \left(1-c_0(t_0)\pi_v(o_{\le k})\right) = \Lambda_{d+1}^{(k+1)}(o).\] This completes the induction, and hence the desired bound for the original prefix of length \(k\).

∎
Lemma 20 [lem:terminal-posterior-mature-window] (Terminal posterior window bound).

Let \(N,Q\in\mathbb N\), let \(t_0,\zeta,C\in\mathbb R\), and let \(v\in\{-1,+1\}\). Put \(\alpha=\exp\{-(1/t_0)\}\). In the finite signed-depth model of Definition 18, for an observed prefix \(o=((a_1,y_1),\ldots,(a_k,y_k))\), write \[\pi_v(o) = \frac{ \sum_{h\in\{0,\ldots,Q\}\times\{-1,+1\}} \mathbf 1\{J(h)=Q\}\lambda_v(h;o) }{ \mathsf M_v(o) }.\] For \(d\in\mathbb N\), define the lower posterior window \[\Lambda_d(o) = \prod_{\substack{0\le i<k\\ k-d\le i}} \left(1-c_0(t_0)\,\pi_v(o_{\le i})\right),\] where \(o_{\le i}\) is the prefix of \(o\) containing its first \(i\) action–reward pairs.

Assume:

  • (Positive scale.) \(t_0>0\).

  • (Positive tilt.) \(\zeta>0\).

  • (Overlap level.) \(C>1\).

  • (Mature window.) \(Q\le k\).

Then the terminal-depth posterior satisfies \[\pi_v(o) \le \frac{\alpha^Q}{\Lambda_Q(o)}.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 20.

Set \(o^-:=o_{\le k-Q}\), and introduce \[\mathsf W_{\mathrm{fair}} := \prod_{k-Q\le i<k} b(a_{i+1})\,\frac12, \qquad \mathsf M_{\mathrm{pre}}:=\mathsf M_v(o^-),\] \[\mathsf U_{\mathrm{pre}} := \sum_{u\in\{-1,+1\}}\lambda_v((0,u);o^-), \qquad \mathsf R_{\mathrm{corr}} := \prod_{k-Q\le i<k} \frac{ \mathsf M_v(o_{\le i+1}) }{ b(a_{i+1})\,\mathsf M_v(o_{\le i}) },\] and \[\mathsf P_{\mathrm{low}}:=\Lambda_Q(o).\] Here the products are over the final \(Q\) action–reward pairs; if \(Q=0\), they are empty products and equal one.

1. The terminal-depth numerator after the full prefix factors as \[\sum_{h\in\{0,\ldots,Q\}\times\{-1,+1\}} \mathbf 1\{J(h)=Q\}\lambda_v(h;o) = \alpha^Q\,\mathsf W_{\mathrm{fair}}\,\mathsf U_{\mathrm{pre}} .\] Indeed, summing over the two terminal signs gives the unsigned mass at depth \(Q\). Over a mature window of length \(Q\), the only way to arrive at terminal depth \(Q\) is to start the window at depth \(0\) and advance once at each of the \(Q\) updates; this contributes \(\alpha^Q\), while the action and sign-symmetric factors over the same window contribute exactly \(\mathsf W_{\mathrm{fair}}\). The remaining mass is the unsigned depth-zero mass \(\mathsf U_{\mathrm{pre}}\) at the beginning of the window.

2. The total mass over the observed prefix factors through the same window as \[\mathsf M_v(o) = \mathsf W_{\mathrm{fair}}\, \mathsf M_{\mathrm{pre}}\, \mathsf R_{\mathrm{corr}} .\] This follows by iterating the one-step mass recursion across the last \(Q\) observations: each step extracts the behavior/fair-sign factor and leaves the corresponding reward-correction factor. For \(Q=0\) the window is empty and the identity reduces to \(\mathsf M_v(o)=\mathsf M_v(o)\).

3. The unsigned depth-zero mass at the window start is bounded by the total mass there: \[\mathsf U_{\mathrm{pre}} = \sum_{u\in\{-1,+1\}}\lambda_v((0,u);o^-) \le \sum_{j=0}^Q\sum_{u\in\{-1,+1\}}\lambda_v((j,u);o^-) = \mathsf M_{\mathrm{pre}} .\] All summands are nonnegative, since each prefix weight is a sum of nonnegative path weights.

4. The lower posterior window is positive and is dominated by the correction window: \[0<\mathsf P_{\mathrm{low}}\le \mathsf R_{\mathrm{corr}} .\] For each factor in \(\mathsf P_{\mathrm{low}}\), \[0\le \pi_v(o_{\le i})\le 1, \qquad 0\le c_0(t_0)<1,\] so \(1-c_0(t_0)\pi_v(o_{\le i})>0\). The one-step reward-correction factor at the same prefix is at least \[1-c_0(t_0)\pi_v(o_{\le i}),\] and taking products over the final window gives the displayed inequality.

5. The remaining factors have the needed signs: \[\mathsf W_{\mathrm{fair}}>0,\qquad \mathsf M_{\mathrm{pre}}>0,\qquad \mathsf R_{\mathrm{corr}}>0,\qquad \alpha^Q\ge 0 .\] The first inequality is the product of positive behavior weights and \(1/2\) factors, while the second is positivity of the finite total mass at the window start. The third follows from Step 4 by \(0<\mathsf P_{\mathrm{low}}\le \mathsf R_{\mathrm{corr}}\). Finally, \(\alpha=\exp\{-(1/t_0)\}>0\), so its \(Q\)-th power is nonnegative.

6. Combining the two window factorizations with the monotonicity bounds gives \[\mathsf U_{\mathrm{pre}}\mathsf P_{\mathrm{low}} \le \mathsf M_{\mathrm{pre}}\mathsf R_{\mathrm{corr}},\] because \(0\le \mathsf U_{\mathrm{pre}}\le \mathsf M_{\mathrm{pre}}\), \(0<\mathsf P_{\mathrm{low}}\le \mathsf R_{\mathrm{corr}}\), and \(\mathsf M_{\mathrm{pre}}\ge0\). Multiplying by the nonnegative factor \(\alpha^Q\mathsf W_{\mathrm{fair}}\) yields \[\alpha^Q\mathsf W_{\mathrm{fair}}\mathsf U_{\mathrm{pre}}\mathsf P_{\mathrm{low}} \le \alpha^Q\mathsf W_{\mathrm{fair}}\mathsf M_{\mathrm{pre}}\mathsf R_{\mathrm{corr}} .\] Using the identities from Steps 1 and 2, this is \[\left( \sum_h \mathbf 1\{J(h)=Q\}\lambda_v(h;o) \right)\mathsf P_{\mathrm{low}} \le \alpha^Q\,\mathsf M_v(o).\] Since \(\mathsf M_v(o)>0\) and \(\mathsf P_{\mathrm{low}}>0\), division gives \[\pi_v(o) = \frac{ \sum_h \mathbf 1\{J(h)=Q\}\lambda_v(h;o) }{ \mathsf M_v(o) } \le \frac{\alpha^Q}{\mathsf P_{\mathrm{low}}} = \frac{\alpha^Q}{\Lambda_Q(o)} ,\] as claimed.

∎
Lemma 21 [lem:terminal-posterior-initial-window] (Initial-window posterior bound).

Let \(N,Q\in\mathbb N\), let \(t_0,\zeta,C\in\mathbb R\), let \(v\in\{-1,+1\}\), let \(k\in\mathbb N\), and let \[o:\{0,\ldots,k-1\}\to \{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}\] be an observed action/reward prefix for the signed-depth construction of Definition 18. Write \(\alpha=e^{-1/t_0}\).
