Proofs of the main results

Proof of Theorem 1.

Write \[\alpha=\exp(-1/t_0),\qquad L=\exp(\zeta),\qquad \beta=\frac{2}{2+t_0\zeta}.\] Then \(0<\alpha<1\), \(1<L\), and \(0<\beta<1\).

Step 1. Adaptive-depth calibration. Set \[\mathcal D=2\log(1/\alpha)+\log L=\frac{2}{t_0}+\zeta, \qquad q=q_C,\qquad \xi=Tq^2 .\] There are \(c_{\mathrm{rad}}>0\) and \(T_{\mathrm{rad}}\ge1\), depending only on \(t_0,\zeta\), such that, for all \(T\ge T_{\mathrm{rad}}\) and all \(C\ge1\), \[q_C^2\alpha^{2\kappa_T^{\mathrm{rad}}(C)} +\frac{L^{\kappa_T^{\mathrm{rad}}(C)}}{T} \le c_{\mathrm{rad}} \left\{ T^{-1}+T^{-\beta}q_C^{2(1-\beta)} \right\}.\] Indeed, since \(\log T=o(T)\), enlarge \(T_{\mathrm{rad}}\) so that \[|\log T|\le \frac{\mathcal D}{2}T \qquad (T\ge T_{\mathrm{rad}}).\] If \(\xi\le1\), then \(\kappa_T^{\mathrm{rad}}(C)=0\) by Definition 20, and \(q_C^2\le T^{-1}\), hence \[q_C^2+\frac1T \le 2T^{-1} \le 2\left\{ T^{-1}+T^{-\beta}q_C^{2(1-\beta)} \right\}.\] If \(\xi>1\), then \(q_C>0\), \(0<\xi\le T\), and the same logarithmic bound gives \[\left\lfloor\frac{\log \xi}{\mathcal D}\right\rfloor\le \frac T2 .\] Thus \(\kappa_T^{\mathrm{rad}}(C)=\lfloor\log\xi/\mathcal D\rfloor\). The floor bounds \[\frac{\log\xi}{\mathcal D}-1 < \kappa_T^{\mathrm{rad}}(C) \le \frac{\log\xi}{\mathcal D}\] give \[\alpha^{2\kappa_T^{\mathrm{rad}}(C)} \le e^{2/t_0}\xi^{-\beta}, \qquad L^{\kappa_T^{\mathrm{rad}}(C)} \le \xi^{1-\beta}.\] Consequently, \[q_C^2\alpha^{2\kappa_T^{\mathrm{rad}}(C)} +\frac{L^{\kappa_T^{\mathrm{rad}}(C)}}{T} \le \{e^{2/t_0}+1\}\,T^{-\beta}q_C^{2(1-\beta)}.\] Taking \(c_{\mathrm{rad}}=\max\{2,e^{2/t_0}+1\}\) proves the calibration.

Step 2. Upper bound. Let \[A=\max\left\{ 4,\, 2L\left(1+\frac4{L-1}\right),\, \frac8{1-\alpha} \right\}, \qquad c_{\mathrm{att}}=A(c_{\mathrm{rad}}+1).\] For \(T\ge T_{\mathrm{rad}}\), put \(k=\kappa_T^{\mathrm{rad}}(C)\). By Definition 20, \(k\le T/2\), and Lemma 34 gives, uniformly over \(M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)\), \[\mathbb E_M[(\widehat\theta_k-\theta(K,e))^2] \le 4q_C^2\alpha^{2k} +\frac2T\left\{ L^{k+1}\left(1+\frac4{L-1}\right)+\frac4{1-\alpha} \right\}.\] By the definition of \(A\), \[4q_C^2\alpha^{2k} +\frac2T\left\{ L^{k+1}\left(1+\frac4{L-1}\right)+\frac4{1-\alpha} \right\} \le A\left\{ q_C^2\alpha^{2k}+\frac{L^k}{T}+\frac1T \right\}.\] The calibration in Step 1 and the inequality \[T^{-1}\le T^{-1}+T^{-\beta}q_C^{2(1-\beta)}\] therefore imply \[\sup_{M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)} \mathbb E_M[(\widehat\theta_{\kappa_T^{\mathrm{rad}}(C)}-\theta(K,e))^2] \le c_{\mathrm{att}}\mathfrak r_T(t_0,\zeta,C).\] Since \(R_T(t_0,\zeta,C)\) is the infimum over observable-data estimators in Definition 14, this also yields \[R_T(t_0,\zeta,C)\le c_{\mathrm{att}}\mathfrak r_T(t_0,\zeta,C).\]

Step 3. Constants for the lower comparison. From Lemma 35, choose \(K_0=K_0(t_0)>0\). With \(c_0=(1-\alpha)/4\), define \[\mathsf B=\frac{4c_0^2K_0^2}{1-c_0^2}>0, \qquad x_\star=\max\left\{1,\frac1{4\mathsf B}\right\}.\] Then \(1<8\mathsf Bx_\star\). Put \[c_{\mathrm{par}}=\frac{e^{-1/4}}{64}, \qquad c_{\mathrm{hid}} = \left\{\frac{c_0(1-\alpha)\alpha}{2}\right\}^2 (8\mathsf B)^{-\beta}\frac{e^{-1/8}}4,\] \[s_\star=1+x_\star^{1-\beta}, \qquad c_{\mathrm{lo}} = \min\left\{\frac{c_{\mathrm{par}}}{2s_\star}, \frac{c_{\mathrm{hid}}}{2}\right\}.\] All these constants are positive and depend only on \(t_0,\zeta\).

Step 4. Lower bound in the small-radius branch. By Lemma 40, \[\frac{c_{\mathrm{par}}}{T}\le R_T(t_0,\zeta,C) \qquad (T\ge1,\ C\ge1).\] If \(\xi=Tq_C^2<x_\star\), then \[T^{-\beta}q_C^{2(1-\beta)} = T^{-1}\xi^{1-\beta} \le T^{-1}x_\star^{1-\beta},\] with both sides equal to \(0\) when \(q_C=0\). Hence \[\mathfrak r_T(t_0,\zeta,C) \le \frac{s_\star}{T},\] and therefore \[c_{\mathrm{lo}}\mathfrak r_T(t_0,\zeta,C) \le \frac{c_{\mathrm{par}}}{T} \le R_T(t_0,\zeta,C).\]

Step 5. Lower bound in the large-radius branch. Assume \(\xi\ge x_\star\). Then \(C>1\). Define \[Q=\left\lceil\frac{\log(8\mathsf B\xi)}{\mathcal D}\right\rceil .\] Because \(8\mathsf B\xi\ge8\mathsf Bx_\star>1\), we have \(Q\ge1\), and \[\mathcal D Q\ge \log(8\mathsf B\xi), \qquad Q<\frac{\log(8\mathsf B\xi)}{\mathcal D}+1 .\] Let \(M_+\) and \(M_-\) be the two signed-depth alternatives of depth \(Q\) from Definition 18, and write \(\theta_+\) and \(\theta_-\) for their target values. By Lemma 18, both alternatives belong to \(\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)\). With \(w_Q\) denoting the terminal depth mass in that construction, the same result gives \[\theta_+=c_0\varepsilon_C w_Q, \qquad \theta_-=-c_0\varepsilon_C w_Q.\] Define the separation radius \[\sigma_Q:=c_0\varepsilon_C w_Q .\] The target-value identities give the separation condition needed for the two-point bound, \[2\sigma_Q\le |\theta_+-\theta_-| .\] Also, by Lemma 35, \[\frac{q_C}{2}\le\varepsilon_C\le q_C\] and, orienting the two observed laws as \(\mathbb P_{Q,+}^{\mathrm{obs},T}\) against \(\mathbb P_{Q,-}^{\mathrm{obs},T}\), \[\operatorname{KL}(\mathbb P_{Q,+}^{\mathrm{obs},T}\,\|\,\mathbb P_{Q,-}^{\mathrm{obs},T}) \le \mathsf B\,T\varepsilon_C^2\alpha^{2Q}L^{-Q}.\] Since \(\alpha^{2Q}L^{-Q}=e^{-\mathcal DQ}\), the choice of \(Q\) gives \[\mathsf B\,T\varepsilon_C^2\alpha^{2Q}L^{-Q} \le \mathsf B\,Tq_C^2(8\mathsf B\,Tq_C^2)^{-1} = \frac18 .\] The two alternatives have the same observed alphabet and the same revealed policies by their construction; the displayed KL bound, in the same orientation, supplies the information and finiteness hypotheses with \(\chi=1/8\). Applying Lemma 39 therefore gives \[R_T(t_0,\zeta,C) \ge \sigma_Q^2\frac{e^{-1/8}}4 = (c_0\varepsilon_Cw_Q)^2\frac{e^{-1/8}}4 .\] The depth mass in Lemma 18 satisfies \[w_Q=\frac{(1-\alpha)\alpha^Q}{1-\alpha^{Q+1}} \ge (1-\alpha)\alpha^Q,\] and the ceiling upper bound implies \[\alpha^Q \ge \alpha(8\mathsf B\xi)^{-1/(t_0\mathcal D)}.\] Thus \[(c_0\varepsilon_Cw_Q)^2\frac{e^{-1/8}}4 \ge c_{\mathrm{hid}}\,q_C^2\xi^{-\beta} = c_{\mathrm{hid}}\,T^{-\beta}q_C^{2(1-\beta)}.\] Combining this hidden lower bound with the parametric floor and using \(c_{\mathrm{lo}}\le c_{\mathrm{par}}/2\) and \(c_{\mathrm{lo}}\le c_{\mathrm{hid}}/2\), we get \[c_{\mathrm{lo}}\mathfrak r_T(t_0,\zeta,C) \le \frac12 R_T(t_0,\zeta,C)+\frac12 R_T(t_0,\zeta,C) = R_T(t_0,\zeta,C).\]

Step 6. Assemble the frontier. Set \[c_{\mathrm{hi}}=\max\{c_{\mathrm{att}},c_{\mathrm{lo}}\}, \qquad T_\star=T_{\mathrm{rad}}.\] Here \(c_{\mathrm{att}}=A(c_{\mathrm{rad}}+1)\) uses the same calibration constant fixed in Step 1. Thus \(0<c_{\mathrm{lo}}\le c_{\mathrm{hi}}\) and \(c_{\mathrm{att}}\le c_{\mathrm{hi}}\). Since \(\mathfrak r_T(t_0,\zeta,C)\ge0\), the two upper bounds from Step 2 with constant \(c_{\mathrm{att}}\) also hold with constant \(c_{\mathrm{hi}}\). Together, Steps 2, 4, and 5 prove, for all \(C\ge1\) and \(T\ge T_\star\), \[c_{\mathrm{lo}}\mathfrak r_T(t_0,\zeta,C) \le R_T(t_0,\zeta,C) \le c_{\mathrm{hi}}\mathfrak r_T(t_0,\zeta,C),\] together with \[\sup_{M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)} \mathbb E_M[ (\widehat\theta_{\kappa_T^{\mathrm{rad}}(C)}-\theta(K,e))^2] \le c_{\mathrm{hi}}\mathfrak r_T(t_0,\zeta,C).\]

Step 7. Immediate estimator in the \(Tq_C^2=O(1)\) regime. Let \(C_T\ge1\) and suppose there is \(D_{\mathrm{imm}}\ge0\) such that, eventually, \[Tq_{C_T}^2\le D_{\mathrm{imm}} .\] Define \[V=L\left(1+\frac4{L-1}\right)+\frac4{1-\alpha}, \qquad c_{\mathrm{imm}}=4D_{\mathrm{imm}}+2V>0.\] For all sufficiently large \(T\), apply Lemma 34 with \(k=0\). Since \(q_{C_T}^2\le D_{\mathrm{imm}}/T\), \[\sup_{M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C_T)} \mathbb E_M[(\widehat\theta_0-\theta(K,e))^2] \le 4q_{C_T}^2+\frac2T V \le \frac{4D_{\mathrm{imm}}+2V}{T} = \frac{c_{\mathrm{imm}}}{T}.\] This is the asserted eventual parametric bound for the immediate estimator.

∎
Proof of Theorem 2.

  1. Set \[\alpha:=\exp(-1/t_0), \qquad L:=\exp(\zeta), \qquad D:=\frac{2}{t_0}+\zeta .\] By \(t_0>0\) and \(\zeta>0\), the constants satisfy \[0<\alpha<1,\qquad L>1,\qquad D>0,\] and, using the definitions in Definitions 7 and 1, \[\log\alpha=-\frac1{t_0}, \qquad \log L=\zeta .\] The exponent in the theorem can be written as \[\beta=\frac{2}{2+t_0\zeta} =\frac{2/t_0}{D}.\] Apply Lemma 15 with \(C\ge1\). It gives constants \(c_{\mathrm{up},0}>0\) and \(T_{\mathrm{up}}\) such that, for all \(T\ge T_{\mathrm{up}}\), \[\sup_{M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)} \mathbb E_M\!\left[ \{\widehat\theta_{k_T}-\theta(M)\}^2 \right] \le c_{\mathrm{up},0}T^{-\beta}.\] For the signed-depth family, the behavior policy in Definition 18 is a memoryless function of the single observed state, independent of the latent coordinate and the past. Consequently, for every horizon \(T\), terminal depth \(Q\ge1\), and sign \(v\in\{-1,+1\}\), the action kernel satisfies Assumption 2. The same alternatives start from the behavior stationary law for every horizon, including the zero-horizon case. Indeed, for \[r:=L^{-1},\] the signed-depth constant-policy stationary law at action-one probability \(r\) has masses \[w_j\frac{1+u\varepsilon_C r^j}{2}, \qquad w_j=\frac{(1-\alpha)\alpha^j}{1-\alpha^{Q+1}},\] and the behavior policy in Definition 18 chooses action one with probability \(L^{-1}=r\). Hence its stationary law is precisely the initial hidden-state law \[w_j\frac{1+u\varepsilon_C L^{-j}}{2}.\] The chronological path law is generated from this initial distribution, so the time-zero joint-state marginal is this stationary law; when \(T=0\), the same identity is the whole marginal check, and for \(T\ge1\) it agrees with the behavior-stationary masses displayed in Lemma 18. Thus Assumption 3 holds for every horizon. These are the two structural hypotheses needed for Lemma 31. Therefore that lemma gives a constant \(B_0>0\), depending on \(t_0,\zeta,C\), such that for all \(T\) and \(Q\ge1\), \[\operatorname{KL}\!\left( \mathbb P_{Q,+1}^{\mathrm{obs},T} \,\middle\|\, \mathbb P_{Q,-1}^{\mathrm{obs},T} \right) \le B_0T\alpha^{2Q}L^{-Q}.\] Let \[\varepsilon_C:=\min\left\{\frac{C-1}{2},\frac12\right\}, \qquad c_0:=\frac{1-\alpha}{4},\] and define \[c_\star:= \{c_0\varepsilon_C(1-\alpha)\alpha\}^2 (8B_0)^{-\beta}\frac{\exp(-1/8)}{4}.\] Since \(C>1\), every factor in this product is positive, so \(c_\star>0\). Finally set \[c^\star:=\max\{c_{\mathrm{up},0},c_\star\}.\] Since \(c_{\mathrm{up},0}>0\) and \(c_\star>0\), the maximum is positive; moreover the second argument is bounded by the maximum. Thus \[0<c^\star, \qquad 0<c_\star\le c^\star .\]

  2. Define \[T_{\mathrm{pos}}:=\left\lceil\frac{1}{8B_0}\right\rceil+1, \qquad T_\star:=\max\{T_{\mathrm{up}},T_{\mathrm{pos}},1\}.\] Fix an integer \(T\ge T_\star\). Then \(T\ge1\), \(T\ge T_{\mathrm{up}}\), and \[1<8B_0T .\] Indeed, \[\frac{1}{8B_0} \le \left\lceil\frac{1}{8B_0}\right\rceil < T,\] and multiplication by \(8B_0>0\) gives the displayed inequality. Put \[\tau_T:=\frac{\log(8B_0T)}{D}, \qquad Q:=\lceil \tau_T\rceil .\] Since \(\log(8B_0T)>0\) and \(D>0\), one has \(Q\ge1\). Moreover, \[\tau_T\le Q<\tau_T+1\] and therefore \[DQ\ge \log(8B_0T).\]

  3. The preceding depth choice makes the two signed-depth alternatives statistically close. First, \[\alpha^{2Q}L^{-Q} = \exp\{Q(2\log\alpha-\log L)\} = \exp(-DQ),\] because \(D=2/t_0+\zeta\), \(\log\alpha=-1/t_0\), and \(\log L=\zeta\). Hence \[\exp(-DQ) \le \exp\{-\log(8B_0T)\} = (8B_0T)^{-1}.\] Combining this inequality with the KL bound from Lemma 31 gives \[\operatorname{KL}\!\left( \mathbb P_{Q,+1}^{\mathrm{obs},T} \,\middle\|\, \mathbb P_{Q,-1}^{\mathrm{obs},T} \right) \le \frac18 .\]

  4. Let \(M_{Q,+1}^T\) and \(M_{Q,-1}^T\) be the two signed-depth experiments of Definition 18. They have one observed state and \(2(Q+1)\) latent states. Their revealed behavior and target policies are common, because the policy part of the construction is independent of the sign \(v\). By Lemma 18, both experiments belong to \(\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)\), and their target values are \[\theta(M_{Q,+1}^T)=c_0\varepsilon_C w_Q, \qquad \theta(M_{Q,-1}^T)=-c_0\varepsilon_C w_Q,\] where \[w_Q := \frac{(1-\alpha)\alpha^Q}{1-\alpha^{Q+1}} .\] Set \[s_T:=c_0\varepsilon_C w_Q .\] Then \(s_T\ge0\), and the two target values satisfy the separation bound \[2s_T \le \bigl|\theta(M_{Q,+1}^T)-\theta(M_{Q,-1}^T)\bigr|.\] The two embedded observed laws have the same finite real-reward support, \[\Omega_T^\circ := \left\{ ((0,a_t,y_t))_{t=1}^T: a_t\in\{0,1\},\ y_t\in\{-1,+1\} \right\}.\] They assign total mass one to \(\Omega_T^\circ\), because the construction has one observed state, Boolean actions, and rewards in \(\{-1,+1\}\). We now check that every atom in this common support has positive mass under both signs. Fix \(o=((0,a_t,y_t))_{t=1}^T\in\Omega_T^\circ\), and choose a fixed latent sign \(u_0\in\{-1,+1\}\). Consider the full hidden lift that keeps the latent state at depth zero and sign \(u_0\): \[S_t=(0,(0,u_0)), \qquad A_t=a_t, \qquad Y_t=y_t, \qquad t=1,\\ldots,T.\] Since \(0<\alpha<1\) and \(Q\ge1\), \[1-\alpha^{Q+1}>0, \qquad w_0=\frac{1-\alpha}{1-\alpha^{Q+1}}>0.\] Also \(0<\varepsilon_C\le1/2\), so the initial mass of the chosen hidden state is positive: \[w_0\frac{1+u_0\varepsilon_C}{2}>0.\] For each action in the observed word, \[b(1\mid0)=\frac1L>0, \qquad b(0\mid0)=1-\frac1L>0,\] because \(L>1\). Along the displayed hidden lift, the current depth is \(0<Q\). Hence the reward factor is \[\frac{1+y_tvc_0u_0\mathbf 1\{0=Q\}}{2}=\frac12>0,\] and the hidden transition that resets to the same depth-zero sign has weight \[(1-\alpha)\frac{1+u_0\varepsilon_C}{2}>0.\] Thus this single compatible hidden lift has strictly positive finite-path probability for each sign \(v\in\{-1,+1\}\), and summing over all lifts gives \[\mathbb P_{Q,v}^{\mathrm{obs},T}(\{o\})>0, \qquad o\in\Omega_T^\circ, \qquad v\in\{-1,+1\}.\] The reward decoding used in the embedding is the same for the two signs, so the embedded laws preserve this common atomic support. Therefore any measurable set with zero mass under one embedded observed law contains no atom of \(\Omega_T^\circ\), and consequently has zero mass under the other embedded observed law. Hence \[\mathbb P_{Q,+1}^{\mathrm{obs},T} \ll \mathbb P_{Q,-1}^{\mathrm{obs},T}, \qquad \mathbb P_{Q,-1}^{\mathrm{obs},T} \ll \mathbb P_{Q,+1}^{\mathrm{obs},T},\] and the finite KL displayed above is an ordinary KL bound between mutually absolutely continuous observed laws.

  5. Apply Lemma 39 to the present pair with \(s=s_T\), taking the \(+1\) alternative as the lemma’s first model and the \(-1\) alternative as its second: \(M_0=M_{Q,+1}^T\) and \(M_1=M_{Q,-1}^T\). With this assignment the lemma’s divergence hypothesis \(\operatorname{KL}(\mathbb P_0\|\mathbb P_1)\le\chi\) reads \[\operatorname{KL}\!\left( \mathbb P_{Q,+1}^{\mathrm{obs},T} \,\middle\|\, \mathbb P_{Q,-1}^{\mathrm{obs},T} \right) \le \frac18 ,\] which is exactly the direction bounded above, and its absolute-continuity hypothesis reads \(\mathbb P_{Q,+1}^{\mathrm{obs},T}\ll\mathbb P_{Q,-1}^{\mathrm{obs},T}\), which the preceding step supplies. The common observable structure was checked in the preceding step, the KL bound gives the information hypothesis with \(\chi=1/8\), and the displayed target-value calculation gives \[2s_T \le \bigl|\theta(M_{Q,+1}^T)-\theta(M_{Q,-1}^T)\bigr|.\] Therefore every measurable observable estimator \(\widehat\theta\), under the stated squared-error integrability conditions for both alternatives, satisfies \[s_T^2\frac{\exp(-1/8)}{4} \le \max\left\{ \mathbb E_{M_{Q,+1}^T}\!\left[ \{\widehat\theta-\theta(M_{Q,+1}^T)\}^2 \right], \mathbb E_{M_{Q,-1}^T}\!\left[ \{\widehat\theta-\theta(M_{Q,-1}^T)\}^2 \right] \right\}.\] Commuting the two entries of the maximum, this is exactly \[s_T^2\frac{\exp(-1/8)}{4} \le \max_{v\in\{-1,+1\}} \mathbb E_{M_{Q,v}^T}\!\left[ \{\widehat\theta-\theta(M_{Q,v}^T)\}^2 \right].\] The two signed-depth alternatives belong to \(\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)\) and share the revealed inputs \((n_X,b,e)\). For the witness clause of the theorem the two squared-error integrability conditions are hypotheses, imposed on the estimator and discharged by whoever supplies it; nothing above derives them. They are derived only when \(\widehat\theta\) is further restricted to the admissible carrier of Definition 14, whose members are measurable and uniformly \([-1,1]\)-valued by definition: for such an estimator the squared error at either alternative is a measurable function bounded by \((1+|\theta_\pm|)^2\le4\) on a probability space, hence integrable, so the preceding pointwise lower bound applies to every admissible estimator. Taking the supremum over the model class, then the infimum over these admissible estimators, yields \[s_T^2\frac{\exp(-1/8)}{4} \le R_T(t_0,\zeta,C).\]

  6. It remains to compare \(s_T\) with \(T^{-\beta/2}\). Since \(0<\alpha<1\) and \(Q\ge1\), also \(Q+1\ge1\). Thus \[0<\alpha^{Q+1}<1,\] so the denominator in the depth mass is strictly positive and satisfies \[0<1-\alpha^{Q+1}\le1.\] The numerator \((1-\alpha)\alpha^Q\) is nonnegative, and division by a positive number at most one gives \[w_Q = \frac{(1-\alpha)\alpha^Q}{1-\alpha^{Q+1}} \ge (1-\alpha)\alpha^Q .\] The upper ceiling inequality \(Q<\tau_T+1\) and the identity \(\log\alpha=-1/t_0\) give \[\alpha^Q = \exp\!\left(-\frac{Q}{t_0}\right) \ge \exp\!\left(-\frac{\tau_T+1}{t_0}\right) = \alpha(8B_0T)^{-(1/t_0)/D}.\] Therefore \[s_T \ge c_0\varepsilon_C(1-\alpha)\alpha (8B_0T)^{-(1/t_0)/D}.\] Squaring and using \(\beta=(2/t_0)/D\) yields \[s_T^2 \ge \{c_0\varepsilon_C(1-\alpha)\alpha\}^2 (8B_0T)^{-\beta} = \{c_0\varepsilon_C(1-\alpha)\alpha\}^2 (8B_0)^{-\beta}T^{-\beta}.\] Multiplying by \(\exp(-1/8)/4\) gives \[c_\star T^{-\beta} \le s_T^2\frac{\exp(-1/8)}{4}.\] Combining this with the two-point floor proves \[c_\star T^{-\beta} \le R_T(t_0,\zeta,C),\] and, for every measurable observable-data estimator satisfying the two integrability conditions, \[c_\star T^{-\beta} \le \max_{v\in\{-1,+1\}} \mathbb E_{M_{Q,v}^T}\!\left[ \{\widehat\theta-\theta(M_{Q,v}^T)\}^2 \right].\]

  7. The upper bound follows from the estimator bound in the first step. Since \(T\ge T_{\mathrm{up}}\), \[\sup_{M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,C)} \mathbb E_M\!\left[ \{\widehat\theta_{k_T}-\theta(M)\}^2 \right] \le c_{\mathrm{up},0}T^{-\beta} \le c^\star T^{-\beta}.\] By Definition 14, the minimax risk is bounded above by the worst-case risk of any admissible observable estimator, in particular by that of \(\widehat\theta_{k_T}\). Thus \[R_T(t_0,\zeta,C) \le c^\star T^{-\beta}.\] Together with the lower bound from the preceding step, this proves all asserted inequalities for every \(T\ge T_\star\), and the chosen \(Q=\lceil \log(8B_0T)/D\rceil\) supplies the stated signed-depth witnesses with one observed state and \(2(Q+1)\) latent states.

∎
Proof of Theorem 3.

Write \[\alpha:=\exp(-1/t_0), \qquad L:=\exp(\zeta).\] Since \(t_0>0\) and \(\zeta>0\), one has \(0<\alpha<1\) and \(1<L\).

1. Define \[c_{\mathrm{imm}} := 2\left\{ L\left(1+\frac{4}{L-1}\right)+\frac{4}{1-\alpha} \right\}.\] The preceding inequalities imply \(c_{\mathrm{imm}}>0\). For any \(T\ge1\), Lemma 34 applied at depth \(k=0\) and overlap constant \(C=1\) gives, uniformly over \(M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,1)\), \[\mathbb E_M\!\left[ \bigl(\widehat\theta_0-\theta(K,e)\bigr)^2 \right] \le \frac{2}{T} \left\{ L\left(1+\frac{4}{L-1}\right)+\frac{4}{1-\alpha} \right\} = \frac{c_{\mathrm{imm}}}{T}.\] Taking the supremum over the model class yields \[\sup_{M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,1)} \mathbb E_M\!\left[ \bigl(\widehat\theta_0-\theta(K,e)\bigr)^2 \right] \le \frac{c_{\mathrm{imm}}}{T}.\]

2. Let \(M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,1)\). Write \(d_b\) and \(d_e\) for the behavior and target stationary laws on the finite joint state space \(\mathcal S\), and define \[\left\|d_b-d_e\right\|_{\mathrm{TV}} := \frac12\sum_{s\in\mathcal S}|d_b(s)-d_e(s)| .\] The stationary-law total-variation bound at overlap radius \(q_C\) gives \[\left\|d_b-d_e\right\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \le q_1,\] and at unit overlap Definition 19 gives \[q_1=\frac{1-1}{1}=0.\] Thus \[\left\|d_b-d_e\right\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \le0.\] Since the displayed total-variation quantity is a half-sum of absolute values, it is nonnegative, so \[\left\|d_b-d_e\right\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=0.\] Unfolding the definition of total variation yields \[\sum_{s\in\mathcal S}|d_b(s)-d_e(s)|=0.\] For each joint state \(s\), the nonnegative summand is bounded by the whole finite sum, \[0\le |d_b(s)-d_e(s)| \le \sum_{u\in\mathcal S}|d_b(u)-d_e(u)| =0,\] and hence \[|d_b(s)-d_e(s)|=0.\] Therefore \(d_b(s)=d_e(s)\) for every \(s\in\mathcal S\), and consequently \(d_e=d_b\).

3. Set \[c_{\mathrm{lo}}:=\frac{\exp(-1/4)}{64}, \qquad c_{\mathrm{hi}}:=\max\{c_{\mathrm{imm}},c_{\mathrm{lo}}\}, \qquad T_\star:=1.\] Then \(c_{\mathrm{lo}}>0\), \(c_{\mathrm{lo}}\le c_{\mathrm{hi}}\), and therefore \(c_{\mathrm{hi}}>0\); also \(T_\star\ge1\). For every \(T\ge T_\star\), Lemma 40 at \(C=1\) gives \[\frac{\exp(-1/4)}{64T} \le R_T(t_0,\zeta,1),\] which is \[\frac{c_{\mathrm{lo}}}{T} \le R_T(t_0,\zeta,1).\] For the upper bound, the minimax risk is bounded above by the worst-case risk of any admissible estimator with nonnegative squared risk; applying this to \(\widehat\theta_0\) and using Step 1 gives \[R_T(t_0,\zeta,1) \le \sup_{M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,1)} \mathbb E_M\!\left[ \bigl(\widehat\theta_0-\theta(K,e)\bigr)^2 \right] \le \frac{c_{\mathrm{imm}}}{T} \le \frac{c_{\mathrm{hi}}}{T}.\]

4. Finally, \[t_0\zeta>0, \qquad\text{hence}\qquad 2+t_0\zeta>2.\] Therefore \[\frac{2}{2+t_0\zeta}<1,\] which is the asserted bound on the hidden-state exponent.

∎
Proof of Theorem 4.

Put \[\alpha=\exp(-1/t_0),\qquad L=\exp(\zeta),\qquad \beta=\frac{2}{2+t_0\zeta},\qquad \gamma=2(1-\beta).\] Since \(t_0>0\) and \(\zeta>0\), one has \(0<\alpha<1\), \(1<L\), \(0<\beta<1\), and hence \(\gamma>0\).

1. A radius-depth calibration. Let \[\Lambda:=2\log(1/\alpha)+\log L=\frac{2}{t_0}+\zeta .\] Then \(\Lambda>0\), \[\beta=\frac{2/t_0}{\Lambda}, \qquad 1-\beta=\frac{\zeta}{\Lambda}.\] There are \(c_R>0\) and \(T_R\ge1\), depending only on \(t_0,\zeta\), such that for every \(T\ge T_R\) and every \(C\ge1\), \[q_C^2\alpha^{2\kappa_T^{\mathrm{rad}}(C)} +\frac{L^{\kappa_T^{\mathrm{rad}}(C)}}{T} \le c_R\left\{ T^{-1}+T^{-\beta}q_C^{2(1-\beta)} \right\}.\] Indeed, choose \(T_R\) so that \(|\log T|\le(\Lambda/2)T\) for all \(T\ge T_R\). Fix such a \(T\), put \(q=q_C\), and set \(x=Tq^2\). The relation \(C\ge1\) and Definition 19 give \(0\le q\le1\), hence \(0\le x\le T\). If \(x\le1\), then \(\kappa_T^{\mathrm{rad}}(C)=0\) by Definition 20, and \(q^2\le T^{-1}\), so \[q^2+\frac1T \le 2T^{-1} \le 2\left\{T^{-1}+T^{-\beta}q^{2(1-\beta)}\right\}.\] If \(x>1\), then \(q>0\). With \[k=\left\lfloor\frac{\log x}{\Lambda}\right\rfloor ,\] the choice of \(T_R\) gives \(k\le T/2\), so the cap in Definition 20 is inactive and \(\kappa_T^{\mathrm{rad}}(C)=k\). The floor bounds \[\frac{\log x}{\Lambda}-1<k\le\frac{\log x}{\Lambda}\] imply \[\alpha^{2k} =\exp\!\left(-\frac{2k}{t_0}\right) \le \exp(2/t_0)x^{-\beta}, \qquad \frac{L^k}{T} \le \frac{x^{1-\beta}}{T}.\] Since \[q^2x^{-\beta} = T^{-\beta}q^{2(1-\beta)}, \qquad \frac{x^{1-\beta}}{T} = T^{-\beta}q^{2(1-\beta)},\] the \(x>1\) case is bounded by \[\bigl(\exp(2/t_0)+1\bigr)T^{-\beta}q^{2(1-\beta)}.\] Taking \[c_R=\max\{2,\exp(2/t_0)+1\}\] proves Equation 5.

2. Constants. Apply Theorem 1 to obtain constants \(c_{\mathrm{lo}},c_{\mathrm{hi}}>0\), with \(c_{\mathrm{lo}}\le c_{\mathrm{hi}}\), and a threshold \(T_U\ge1\), all depending only on \(t_0,\zeta\), such that its two-sided risk comparison and radius-calibrated estimator bound hold for all \(C\ge1\) and \(T\ge T_U\). Define \[\eta_{\mathrm{rad}}:=2^{-\gamma},\] and \[\mathsf A_{\mathrm{bd}} := \max\left\{ 4,\, 2L\left(1+\frac{4}{L-1}\right),\, \frac{8}{1-\alpha} \right\}.\] Finally set \[c_{\mathrm{loc}}:=c_{\mathrm{lo}}\eta_{\mathrm{rad}}, \qquad c_{\mathrm{att}}:=\mathsf A_{\mathrm{bd}}(c_R+1), \qquad C_{\mathrm{loc}}:=\max\{c_{\mathrm{hi}},c_{\mathrm{att}}\}.\] Then \(0<c_{\mathrm{loc}}\le C_{\mathrm{loc}}\), because \(0<\eta_{\mathrm{rad}}\le1\), \(c_{\mathrm{lo}}\le c_{\mathrm{hi}}\), and \(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{bd}}>0\).

3. Comparing the two rate surfaces. Fix a deterministic sequence \((\delta_T)_{T\ge1}\) with \(\delta_T\ge0\) and \(\delta_T\to0\). Choose \(T_\Delta\) such that \[0\le\delta_T<1\qquad\text{for all }T\ge T_\Delta .\] Let \(T_\star=\max\{T_U,T_R,T_\Delta\}\). For \(T\ge T_\star\), put \(C_T=1+\delta_T\). Then \(C_T\ge1\), and \[q_{C_T} = \frac{C_T-1}{C_T} = \frac{\delta_T}{1+\delta_T}.\] Since \(0\le\delta_T<1\), \[0\le q_{C_T}\le\delta_T, \qquad \frac{\delta_T}{2}\le q_{C_T}.\] Raising these inequalities to the positive power \(\gamma\) gives \[\eta_{\mathrm{rad}}\delta_T^\gamma \le q_{C_T}^\gamma \le \delta_T^\gamma .\] Thus the theorem’s local rate, evaluated along the fixed sequence \((\delta_T)\), and the fixed-radius rate at \(C_T\) satisfy \[\eta_{\mathrm{rad}}\left\{T^{-1}+T^{-\beta}\delta_T^\gamma\right\} \le \mathfrak r_T(t_0,\zeta,C_T) \le T^{-1}+T^{-\beta}\delta_T^\gamma.\] Equivalently, since \(r_T(\delta)\) in the statement denotes this fixed-sequence quantity, \[\eta_{\mathrm{rad}}\,r_T(\delta) \le \mathfrak r_T(t_0,\zeta,C_T) \le r_T(\delta).\] Combining Equation 6 with the two-sided comparison from Theorem 1 gives \[c_{\mathrm{loc}}\,r_T(\delta) \le R_T(t_0,\zeta,1+\delta_T) \le C_{\mathrm{loc}}\,r_T(\delta).\]

4. Attainment by the displayed shrinking depth. For \(T\ge T_\star\), define \[C_T^\dagger:=\frac{1}{1-\delta_T}.\] Then \(C_T^\dagger\ge1\), and \[q_{C_T^\dagger} = \frac{C_T^\dagger-1}{C_T^\dagger} = \delta_T .\] Therefore Definition 20 gives \[\kappa_T^{\mathrm{rad}}(C_T^\dagger)=\kappa_T.\] where \(\kappa_T\) is the depth displayed in the theorem statement. In particular \(\kappa_T\le T/2\). Applying Equation 5 with \(C=C_T^\dagger\) yields \[\delta_T^2\alpha^{2\kappa_T} +\frac{L^{\kappa_T}}{T} \le c_R r_T(\delta).\] Now fix \(M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,1+\delta_T)\). By Lemma 34, the estimator from Definition 16 satisfies \[\mathbb E_M\!\left[ \bigl(\widehat\theta_{\kappa_T}-\theta(M)\bigr)^2 \right] \le 4q_{C_T}^2\alpha^{2\kappa_T} +\frac{2}{T} \left\{ L^{\kappa_T+1}\left(1+\frac{4}{L-1}\right) +\frac{4}{1-\alpha} \right\}.\] Using \(q_{C_T}\le\delta_T\) and the definition of \(\mathsf A_{\mathrm{bd}}\), \[\mathbb E_M\!\left[ \bigl(\widehat\theta_{\kappa_T}-\theta(M)\bigr)^2 \right] \le \mathsf A_{\mathrm{bd}} \left\{ \delta_T^2\alpha^{2\kappa_T} +\frac{L^{\kappa_T}}{T} +T^{-1} \right\}.\] Together with Equation 9 and \(T^{-1}\le r_T(\delta)\), this gives \[\mathbb E_M\!\left[ \bigl(\widehat\theta_{\kappa_T}-\theta(M)\bigr)^2 \right] \le c_{\mathrm{att}}\,r_T(\delta) \le C_{\mathrm{loc}}\,r_T(\delta).\] Taking the supremum over \(M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,1+\delta_T)\) proves the asserted estimator bound.

5. The bounded local regime. Assume that there is a constant \(D_{\mathrm{bd}}\ge0\) such that \[T\delta_T^2\le D_{\mathrm{bd}}\] for all sufficiently large \(T\). Since \(q_{1+\delta_T}\le\delta_T\), the same tail of \(T\)’s satisfies \[Tq_{1+\delta_T}^2\le D_{\mathrm{bd}}.\] Applying the final assertion of Theorem 1 to the sequence \(C_T=1+\delta_T\) gives a constant \(c_{\mathrm{imm}}>0\) such that, for all sufficiently large \(T\), \[\sup_{M\in\mathcal M_T(t_0,\zeta,1+\delta_T)} \mathbb E_M\!\left[ \bigl(\widehat\theta_0-\theta(M)\bigr)^2 \right] \le \frac{c_{\mathrm{imm}}}{T}.\] This is the claimed immediate-depth conclusion.

∎
Proof of Lemma 1.

Fix \(T\), a policy \(p\), and probability vectors \(\nu,\nu'\) on \(\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}\). We prove the displayed total-variation bound; the \(\ell^1\) formulation follows from \(\|\eta\|_1=2\|\eta\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\).

1. Let \(\mathsf U(s')=1/360\) be the uniform law on \(\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}\). For \(s=(g,d_1,d_2,x_1,x_2,a_1)\), \(a\in\{0,1\}\), and \(s'=(g',d'_1,d'_2,x'_1,x'_2,a'_1)\), define the non-refresh transition weight \[\mathsf D(s,a;s') = \gamma_{s,a}(g')\, \pi_d(d'_1)\, \pi_x(x'_1)\, \mathbf 1\{d'_2=d_1\}\, \mathbf 1\{x'_2=x_1\}\, \mathbf 1\{a'_1=a\},\] where \(\pi_d(0)=4/5\), \(\pi_d(78)=1/5\), \(\pi_x(0)=2/5\), \(\pi_x(31)=2/5\), \(\pi_x(850)=1/5\), and \[\gamma_{s,a}(g^\star) = \Pr\!\left\{ \operatorname{round}_{\{50,100,150,200,250\}} \operatorname{clip}_{[50,250]} \bigl( 10+0.9g+0.1d_1+0.1d_2-0.01x_1-0.01x_2-2a-4a_1+\xi \bigr) =g^\star \right\}\] for \(\xi\sim N(0,25)\). The refresh construction in Definition 24 gives, after marginalizing over the reward, \[K_T\{(q,s''):\ s''=s'\mid s,a\} = \frac{1}{720}+\frac12\,\mathsf D(s,a;s').\] Therefore the policy-induced matrix can be written as \[P_p^{(T)}(s,s') = \frac{1}{720} +\frac12 R_p(s,s'), \qquad R_p(s,s') = \sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}p(a\mid x(s))\,\mathsf D(s,a;s').\] Equivalently, \[P_p^{(T)}(s,s') = \Bigl(1-\frac12\Bigr)\mathsf U(s') +\frac12 R_p(s,s').\]

2. For each fixed \(s\), \(R_p(s,\cdot)\) is a probability vector. Indeed, all factors in \(\mathsf D(s,a;s')\) are nonnegative. Also, \[\sum_{s'\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}}\mathsf D(s,a;s') = \sum_{g^\star}\gamma_{s,a}(g^\star) \sum_{d^\star}\pi_d(d^\star) \sum_{x^\star}\pi_x(x^\star) =1,\] because the rounded Gaussian cells partition the clipped glucose outcome, the diet probabilities sum to \(1\), the activity probabilities sum to \(1\), and the three indicator factors merely select the shifted memory coordinates. Thus \[\sum_{s'\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}} R_p(s,s') = \sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}p(a\mid x(s)) \sum_{s'\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}}\mathsf D(s,a;s') = \sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}p(a\mid x(s)) =1.\]

3. It remains to apply the common-refresh contraction calculation. For any finite state space, any probability vectors \(\eta,\eta'\), and any stochastic matrix \(R\), a kernel of the form \[M_\alpha(s,t)=(1-\alpha)u(t)+\alpha R(s,t), \qquad \alpha\ge 0,\] satisfies \[(\eta M_\alpha-\eta'M_\alpha)(t) = \alpha\sum_s(\eta(s)-\eta'(s))R(s,t),\] since the refresh term contributes \((1-\alpha)u(t)(\sum_s\eta(s)-\sum_s\eta'(s))=0\). Hence \[\begin{aligned} \|\eta M_\alpha-\eta'M_\alpha\|_{\mathrm{TV}} &= \frac12\sum_t \left| \alpha\sum_s(\eta(s)-\eta'(s))R(s,t) \right| \\ &\le \alpha\,\frac12\sum_s|\eta(s)-\eta'(s)| \sum_t R(s,t) \\ &= \alpha\|\eta-\eta'\|_{\mathrm{TV}} . \end{aligned}\] With \(\alpha=1/2\), \(u=\mathsf U\), \(R=R_p\), and \(\eta=\nu,\eta'=\nu'\), this gives \[\|\nu P_p^{(T)}-\nu'P_p^{(T)}\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \le \frac12\|\nu-\nu'\|_{\mathrm{TV}} .\] Multiplying by \(2\) yields the stated \(\ell^1\) version.

∎
Proof of Lemma 2.

Write \[P=P_p^{(T)}\] for the transition matrix induced by the policy \(p\) on the finite state space \(\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}\). We verify the hypotheses of the finite-state contraction fixed-point criterion and then identify the selected fixed point.

  1. Since \(p(\cdot\mid x)\) is a probability law on \(\{0,1\}\) for each observed grid state \(x\), and since the kernel of \(\mathcal I_{\mathrm{grid}}\) is a Markov kernel, the policy mixture \[P(s,s') = \sum_{a\in\{0,1\}} p(a\mid x(s))\,K_T(s,a;s')\] is stochastic. Thus, for every probability vector \(\nu\) on \(\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}\), \[(\nu P)(s')=\sum_{s\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}}\nu(s)P(s,s')\] is again a probability vector on \(\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}\). The initial law specified for \(\mathcal I_{\mathrm{grid}}\) is also a probability vector.

  2. By the stochasticity verified in the preceding step, the map \(\nu\mapsto \nu P\) sends the probability simplex into itself. The displayed hypothesis of Lemma 2 gives \[\|\nu P-\nu'P\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \le \frac12\|\nu-\nu'\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\] for all probability vectors \(\nu,\nu'\). The constant \(1/2\) is nonnegative and strictly smaller than \(1\). Since the probability simplex over the finite set \(\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}\) is complete in total variation distance, the contraction mapping theorem gives a probability vector \(d\) such that \[dP=d .\] Equivalently, \[d(s')=\sum_{s\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}}d(s)P(s,s'), \qquad s'\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}} .\]

  3. The selected law \(d_p\) is defined as the stationary law chosen for \(P\) whenever such a stationary probability vector exists. The preceding step supplies that existence, so the selected law is itself a probability vector and satisfies \[d_pP=d_p .\] Reading this coordinatewise yields \[d_p(s') = \sum_{s\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}}d_p(s)P_p^{(T)}(s,s'), \qquad s'\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}} ,\] which is the asserted stationarity equation.

∎
Proof of Lemma 3.

Fix \(s=(g,d_1,d_2,x_1,x_2,a_1)\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}\) and abbreviate \[g_e(s) := \sum_{a\in\{0,1\}} e(a\mid x(s))\int p_1\,K_T(dp\mid s,a).\] By the definition of the target-policy reward regression, the left-hand side of the claimed identity is \(g_e(s)\).

For each \(a\in\{0,1\}\), the embedded insulin kernel assigns the reward coordinate according to the current glucose component of \(s\). Hence its conditional reward mean is \[\int p_1\,K_T(dp\mid s,a) = r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s).\] Indeed, Definition 24 makes the reward coordinate a deterministic function of the present state, with the displayed values defining \(r_{\mathrm{ins}}\); the subsequent randomization affects the next state coordinates but not this reward coordinate.

Substituting this identity into the regression gives \[g_e(s) = \sum_{a\in\{0,1\}} e(a\mid x(s))\,r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s) = \left(\sum_{a\in\{0,1\}} e(a\mid x(s))\right) r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s).\] The target policy in Definition 24 is a probability vector on \(\{0,1\}\): if \(g\ge 125\), then \(e(1\mid x(s))=1\) and \(e(0\mid x(s))=0\), while if \(g<125\), then \(e(1\mid x(s))=0\) and \(e(0\mid x(s))=1\). Therefore \[\sum_{a\in\{0,1\}} e(a\mid x(s))=1,\] and consequently \(g_e(s)=r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s)\).

This is the asserted identity.

∎
Proof of Lemma 4.

Fix \(s=(g,d_1,d_2,x_1,x_2,a_1)\) and write \(x=x(s)\).

  1. Let \(\operatorname{cat}_{\mathrm{rew}}(s)\in\{0,1,2,3\}\) be the reward category selected by the current glucose coordinate, and let \[\operatorname{val}_{\mathrm{rew}}(0)=-1,\qquad \operatorname{val}_{\mathrm{rew}}(1)=-\frac23,\qquad \operatorname{val}_{\mathrm{rew}}(2)=-\frac13,\qquad \operatorname{val}_{\mathrm{rew}}(3)=0 .\] Thus \(\operatorname{val}_{\mathrm{rew}}(\operatorname{cat}_{\mathrm{rew}}(s))=r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s)\). In the finite insulin kernel of Definition 24, for each action \(a\) the next-state mass may be written as \(\omega_{s,a}(\sigma)\), with \[\omega_{s,a}(\sigma)\ge 0, \qquad \sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}}\omega_{s,a}(\sigma)=1,\] and the reward coordinate is deterministic at the current-state reward category: \[K_T\bigl(\{(\operatorname{val}_{\mathrm{rew}}(\operatorname{cat}_{\mathrm{rew}}(s)),\sigma): \sigma\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}\}\mid s,a\bigr)=1 .\] Therefore finite-space integration gives \[\begin{aligned} \int p_1\,K_T(dp\mid s,a) &= \sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}} \omega_{s,a}(\sigma)\, \operatorname{val}_{\mathrm{rew}}(\operatorname{cat}_{\mathrm{rew}}(s)) \\ &= \operatorname{val}_{\mathrm{rew}}(\operatorname{cat}_{\mathrm{rew}}(s)) \sum_{\sigma\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}}\omega_{s,a}(\sigma) = r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s). \end{aligned}\]

  2. The behavior policy in Definition 24 satisfies \[b(1\mid x)=\frac{3}{10}, \qquad b(0\mid x)=\frac{7}{10},\] so \(b(a\mid x)>0\) for both \(a\in\{0,1\}\). By the zero-cell convention in the definition of \(\rho\), the positive-denominator branch applies and hence \[b(a\mid x)\rho(x,a) = b(a\mid x)\frac{e(a\mid x)}{b(a\mid x)} = e(a\mid x).\]

  3. Substituting the two preceding identities into the weighted regression gives \[\begin{aligned} \sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}b(a\mid x)\rho(x,a) \int p_1\,K_T(dp\mid s,a) &= \sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}e(a\mid x)\,r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s)\\ &= \left(\sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}e(a\mid x)\right)r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s). \end{aligned}\] The target policy in Definition 24 injects exactly when \(g\ge125\), so \[e(1\mid x)=\mathbf 1\{g\ge125\}, \qquad e(0\mid x)=1-\mathbf 1\{g\ge125\}, \qquad \sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}e(a\mid x)=1 .\] Consequently \[\sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}b(a\mid x)\rho(x,a) \int p_1\,K_T(dp\mid s,a) = r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s),\] as claimed.

∎
Proof of Lemma 5.

  1. Fix \(k=0,\ldots,m\) and \(s'\in\mathsf S\). For this pair \((k,s')\), write the chunked-recurrence data as \[\mathcal G^{k,s'}_1,\ldots,\mathcal G^{k,s'}_{J_{k,s'}}, \qquad \Xi^{k,s'}_1(s'),\ldots,\Xi^{k,s'}_{J_{k,s'}}(s'), \qquad \Sigma^{k,s'}_1,\ldots,\Sigma^{k,s'}_{J_{k,s'}+1}.\] These are the block partition, block intervals, and partial-sum intervals attached to this fixed \((k,s')\). The chunked recurrence first gives the ordinary interval recurrence \[\sum_{s\in\mathsf S}\mathfrak T_k(s)\cdot\mathfrak A(s,s') \subseteq \mathfrak T_{k+1}(s').\] Indeed, take arbitrary choices from the interval summands on the left and group them by the disjoint covering blocks \(\mathcal G^{k,s'}_1,\ldots,\mathcal G^{k,s'}_{J_{k,s'}}\). The contribution of block \(\mathcal G^{k,s'}_j\) lies in \(\Xi^{k,s'}_j(s')\). Starting from the tail value \(0\in\Sigma^{k,s'}_{J_{k,s'}+1}\), the inclusions \[\Xi^{k,s'}_j(s')+\Sigma^{k,s'}_{j+1}\subseteq \Sigma^{k,s'}_j, \qquad j=J_{k,s'},\ldots,1,\] show by backward induction that the sum of the block contributions from \(j\) through \(J_{k,s'}\) lies in \(\Sigma^{k,s'}_j\). At \(j=1\) this is the full sum, and \(\Sigma^{k,s'}_1=\mathfrak T_{k+1}(s')\). If the block list is empty, the same argument is just the terminal condition \(0\in\Sigma^{k,s'}_1=\mathfrak T_{k+1}(s')\). Thus the initial row together with these coordinate recurrences is the ordinary finite-iterate certificate with rows \(\mathfrak T_0,\ldots,\mathfrak T_{m+1}\) used in the remaining argument.

  2. Define the true iterates \(\eta_n:\mathsf S\to\mathbb R\), for all \(n\in\mathbb N\), by \[\eta_{0}(s)=\mu_0(s),\qquad \eta_{n+1}(s')=\sum_{s\in\mathsf S}\eta_n(s)P(s,s').\] Here \(\mu_0\) is the given initial probability vector, while \(\eta_n\) denotes its \(n\)-step image under \(P\). The initial condition gives \(\eta_0(s)\in\mathfrak T_0(s)\). If \(\eta_n(s)\in\mathfrak T_n(s)\) for every \(s\), then \(P(s,s')\in\mathfrak A(s,s')\) and exact interval multiplication give \[\eta_n(s)P(s,s')\in \mathfrak T_n(s)\cdot\mathfrak A(s,s'),\] and exact interval summation together with the recurrence from the previous step gives \[\eta_{n+1}(s')\in\mathfrak T_{n+1}(s').\] Thus, for \(n=0,\ldots,m+1\), \[\eta_n(s)\in\mathfrak T_n(s)\qquad\text{for every }s\in\mathsf S.\] In particular, \[\eta_m(s)\in\mathfrak T_m(s),\qquad \eta_{m+1}(s)\in\mathfrak T_{m+1}(s).\] Since \(\mu_0\) is a probability vector and \(P\) is stochastic, every \(\eta_n\) is a probability vector.

  3. The terminal interval expectation contains the true reward expectation at time \(m\): \[\sum_{s\in\mathsf S}\eta_m(s)r(s) \in \sum_{s\in\mathsf S}\mathfrak T_m(s)\cdot\mathfrak r(s) =\mathfrak E.\] This follows coordinatewise from \(\eta_m(s)\in\mathfrak T_m(s)\) and \(r(s)\in\mathfrak r(s)\), followed by exact interval products and exact finite interval addition.

  4. The one-step residual of \(\eta_m\) is bounded by \(\delta\). Since \(\eta_{m+1}=\eta_mP\), exact interval subtraction gives \[\eta_m(s)-\eta_{m+1}(s) \in \mathfrak T_m(s)\ominus\mathfrak T_{m+1}(s),\] and hence \[|\eta_m(s)-\eta_{m+1}(s)| \le \bigl|\mathfrak T_m(s)\ominus\mathfrak T_{m+1}(s)\bigr|_{\max}.\] Summing over \(s\) yields \[\|\eta_m-\eta_mP\|_1 =\|\eta_m-\eta_{m+1}\|_1 \le \delta.\] Because \(\pi\) is stationary and both \(\eta_m\) and \(\pi\) are probability vectors, \[\begin{aligned} \|\eta_m-\pi\|_1 &\le \|\eta_m-\eta_mP\|_1+\|\eta_mP-\pi P\|_1\\ &\le \delta+\rho\|\eta_m-\pi\|_1 . \end{aligned}\] Since \(1-\rho>0\), this gives \[\|\eta_m-\pi\|_1\le \frac{\delta}{1-\rho}.\]

  5. For every state \(s\), \[|r(s)| \le \max\{|\ell(\mathfrak r(s))|,|u(\mathfrak r(s))|\} =|\mathfrak r(s)|_{\max} \le B.\] Therefore \[\begin{aligned} \left| \sum_{s\in\mathsf S}\pi(s)r(s) - \sum_{s\in\mathsf S}\eta_m(s)r(s) \right| &= \left|\sum_{s\in\mathsf S}(\pi(s)-\eta_m(s))r(s)\right|\\ &\le \sum_{s\in\mathsf S}|\pi(s)-\eta_m(s)|\,|r(s)|\\ &\le B\|\pi-\eta_m\|_1\\ &\le \frac{B\delta}{1-\rho}. \end{aligned}\]

  6. Combining the preceding two enclosures, let \[x_m=\sum_{s\in\mathsf S}\eta_m(s)r(s), \qquad x_\pi=\sum_{s\in\mathsf S}\pi(s)r(s).\] From \(x_m\in\mathfrak E\) and \[|x_\pi-x_m|\le \frac{B\delta}{1-\rho},\] we obtain \[\ell(\mathfrak E)-\frac{B\delta}{1-\rho} \le x_\pi\le u(\mathfrak E)+\frac{B\delta}{1-\rho}.\] By the definition of \(\mathfrak J\), this is exactly \[\sum_{s\in\mathsf S}\pi(s)r(s)\in\mathfrak J .\]

∎
Proof of Lemma 6.

All arithmetic below is exact rational interval arithmetic with the operations described in Lemma 5.

  1. The residual sums over the \(360\) states of Definition 24 evaluate to \[\delta_{\mathrm b} = \sum_{s\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}} \bigl|\mathfrak T_{\mathrm b,0}(s)\ominus \mathfrak T_{\mathrm b,1}(s)\bigr|_{\max} = \frac{2596399843}{200000000000000},\] and \[\delta_{\mathrm e} = \sum_{s\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}} \bigl|\mathfrak T_{\mathrm e,0}(s)\ominus \mathfrak T_{\mathrm e,1}(s)\bigr|_{\max} = \frac{16950307061}{1000000000000000}.\]

  2. Since \(\mathfrak r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s)=[r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s),r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s)]\), the interval expectations computed from the terminal rows are \[\mathfrak E_{\mathrm b} = \sum_{s\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}} \mathfrak T_{\mathrm b,0}(s)\cdot\mathfrak r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s) = \left[ -\frac{157888864566941}{300000000000000}, -\frac{63155545824091}{120000000000000} \right],\] and \[\mathfrak E_{\mathrm e} = \sum_{s\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}} \mathfrak T_{\mathrm e,0}(s)\cdot\mathfrak r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s) = \left[ -\frac{312236715379949}{600000000000000}, -\frac{312236715366659}{600000000000000} \right].\]

  3. Because \(1-1/2=1/2\), the expansion radius in each case is \(2\delta_\star\). Hence \[\mathfrak J_{\mathrm b} = \left[ -\frac{157888864566941}{300000000000000} -2\frac{2596399843}{200000000000000}, -\frac{63155545824091}{120000000000000} +2\frac{2596399843}{200000000000000} \right] = \left[ -\frac{15789665376647}{30000000000000}, -\frac{315762150721397}{600000000000000} \right],\] and \[\mathfrak J_{\mathrm e} = \left[ -\frac{312236715379949}{600000000000000} -2\frac{16950307061}{1000000000000000}, -\frac{312236715366659}{600000000000000} +2\frac{16950307061}{1000000000000000} \right] = \left[ -\frac{1561285278742111}{3000000000000000}, -\frac{1561081874990929}{3000000000000000} \right].\]

  4. Taking the interval difference, \[\mathfrak B = \mathfrak J_{\mathrm b}\ominus\mathfrak J_{\mathrm e} = \left[ \ell(\mathfrak J_{\mathrm b})-u(\mathfrak J_{\mathrm e}), u(\mathfrak J_{\mathrm b})-\ell(\mathfrak J_{\mathrm e}) \right],\] so \[\mathfrak B = \left[ -\frac{17884662673771}{3000000000000000}, -\frac{2920912477479}{500000000000000} \right].\] The endpoint comparisons are then the rational inequalities \[-\frac{6}{1000} = -\frac{18000000000000}{3000000000000000} < -\frac{17884662673771}{3000000000000000} = \ell(\mathfrak B),\] and \[u(\mathfrak B) = -\frac{2920912477479}{500000000000000} < -\frac{2900000000000}{500000000000000} = -\frac{58}{10000}.\] This proves both asserted endpoint bounds.

∎
Proof of Lemma 7.

Fix \(T\in\mathbb N\). Write \(P_b^{(T)}\) and \(P_e^{(T)}\) for the policy-induced transition matrices on \(\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}\) obtained from Lemma 1 by taking \(p=b\) and \(p=e\), respectively, and write \(d_b^{(T)}\) and \(d_e^{(T)}\) for their selected stationary laws.

  1. The behavior and target kernels both satisfy the one-half contraction estimate \[\|\nu P_b^{(T)}-\nu'P_b^{(T)}\|_1 \le {1\over2}\|\nu-\nu'\|_1, \qquad \|\nu P_e^{(T)}-\nu'P_e^{(T)}\|_1 \le {1\over2}\|\nu-\nu'\|_1\] for all probability vectors \(\nu,\nu'\) on \(\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}\). This is exactly the \(\ell^1\)-form supplied by Lemma 1, instantiated first at \(p=b\) and then at \(p=e\). The corresponding selected laws are probability vectors and satisfy \[d_b^{(T)}P_b^{(T)}=d_b^{(T)}, \qquad d_e^{(T)}P_e^{(T)}=d_e^{(T)},\] by Lemma 2.

  2. Let \[r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s)= \begin{cases} -1,&g\le70,\\ -2/3,&g>150,\\ -1/3,&70<g\le80\ \text{or}\ 120<g\le150,\\ 0,&\text{otherwise}, \end{cases} \qquad s=(g,d_1,d_2,x_1,x_2,a_1).\] For \(\star\in\{\mathrm b,\mathrm e\}\), with \(P_\star^{(T)}\) denoting \(P_b^{(T)}\) or \(P_e^{(T)}\), let \(\mathfrak A_\star\) be the exact rational interval matrix recorded by the finite-grid certificate. Its entrywise enclosure is \[P_\star^{(T)}(s,s')\in \mathfrak A_\star(s,s'), \qquad s,s'\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}.\] The same certificate supplies an exact rational initial probability row \(\mu_{\star,0}\), interval rows \(\mathfrak T_{\star,0}\) and \(\mathfrak T_{\star,1}\), and an exact chunked recurrence from the first interval row to the second. In the notation of Lemma 5, its rational inclusions verify \[[\mu_{\star,0}(s),\mu_{\star,0}(s)] \subseteq \mathfrak T_{\star,0}(s), \qquad s\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}.\] For every output state, the recorded blocks partition \(\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}\), every block has at most \(40\) states, and the block and partial-sum intervals satisfy exactly the inclusion chain in Lemma 5. Because the certificate has zero approximation steps, this verified recurrence links its initial interval row directly to its successor row. Passing from the chunked certificate to the finite recurrence used by that lemma preserves both rows and all the rational inclusions. For the reward certificate, put \[\mathfrak r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s)=[r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s),r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s)].\] Since \(|r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s)|\le1\) for every grid state, the reward bound in Lemma 5 is \(B=1\). Define \[\delta_\star = \sum_{s\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}} \bigl| \mathfrak T_{\star,0}(s)\ominus \mathfrak T_{\star,1}(s) \bigr|_{\max}, \qquad \mathfrak E_\star = \sum_{s\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}} \mathfrak T_{\star,0}(s)\cdot \mathfrak r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s) .\] After that conversion, the terminal row is \(\mathfrak T_{\star,0}\) and the successor row is \(\mathfrak T_{\star,1}\), so the residual and stationary-reward interval are \[\delta_\star = \sum_{s\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}} \bigl| \mathfrak T_{\star,0}(s)\ominus \mathfrak T_{\star,1}(s) \bigr|_{\max}, \qquad \mathfrak J_\star = \left[ \ell(\mathfrak E_\star)-{\delta_\star\over 1-1/2}, u(\mathfrak E_\star)+{\delta_\star\over 1-1/2} \right].\] The initial-row, recurrence, reward-bound, contraction, and stationarity hypotheses of Lemma 5 are therefore satisfied for both policies after passing each chunked recurrence certificate to its associated finite recurrence certificate. Under this conversion, the terminal interval row is \(\mathfrak T_{\star,0}\), the successor interval row is \(\mathfrak T_{\star,1}\), and the stationary-reward interval produced by Lemma 5 is exactly \[\mathfrak J_\star = \left[ \ell(\mathfrak E_\star)-{\delta_\star\over 1-1/2}, u(\mathfrak E_\star)+{\delta_\star\over 1-1/2} \right],\] which is the chunked interval \(\mathfrak J_\star\) used here. Hence \[\sum_{s\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}} d_b^{(T)}(s)r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s)\in\mathfrak J_{\mathrm b}, \qquad \sum_{s\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}} d_e^{(T)}(s)r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s)\in\mathfrak J_{\mathrm e}.\]

  3. Define the subtraction interval \[\mathfrak B:=\mathfrak J_{\mathrm b}\ominus\mathfrak J_{\mathrm e} = \bigl[ \ell(\mathfrak J_{\mathrm b})-u(\mathfrak J_{\mathrm e}), u(\mathfrak J_{\mathrm b})-\ell(\mathfrak J_{\mathrm e}) \bigr].\] Interval subtraction is sound, so the two inclusions from the previous step give \[\sum_s d_b^{(T)}(s)r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s) - \sum_s d_e^{(T)}(s)r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s) \in \mathfrak B .\]

  4. The reward-regression identities in Lemmas 4 and 3 give, for every \(s\in\mathsf S_{\mathrm{grid}}\), \[\sum_{a\in\{0,1\}} b(a\mid x(s))\,\rho(x(s),a) \int p_1\,K_T(dp\mid s,a) = r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s),\] and \[\sum_{a\in\{0,1\}} e(a\mid x(s)) \int p_1\,K_T(dp\mid s,a) = r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s).\] The finite-grid embedding preserves the policy-induced kernels entry by entry: \[P_b^{(T)}(s,s')= \sum_a b(a\mid x(s))K_T(\mathbb R\times\{s'\}\mid s,a), \qquad P_e^{(T)}(s,s')= \sum_a e(a\mid x(s))K_T(\mathbb R\times\{s'\}\mid s,a).\] Consequently the stationary laws in the interval certificate are exactly the behavior- and target-policy stationary laws entering the two regression identities. Combining these kernel identifications, the two regression identities, and Definition 12 yields \[\Delta_{\mathrm{imm}}(T) = \sum_s d_b^{(T)}(s)r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s) - \sum_s d_e^{(T)}(s)r_{\mathrm{ins}}(s).\] Therefore \[\Delta_{\mathrm{imm}}(T)\in\mathfrak B .\]

  5. By the exact rational interval evaluation in Lemma 6, the endpoints of the subtraction interval \(\mathfrak B\) satisfy \[-\frac{6}{1000}<\ell(\mathfrak B), \qquad u(\mathfrak B)<-\frac{58}{10000}.\] Since \(\Delta_{\mathrm{imm}}(T)\in\mathfrak B\), we have \[\ell(\mathfrak B) \le \Delta_{\mathrm{imm}}(T) \le u(\mathfrak B).\] The last two displays imply \[-\frac{6}{1000} < \Delta_{\mathrm{imm}}(T) < -\frac{58}{10000},\] as claimed.

∎
Proof of Theorem 5.

Fix \(T\). We verify the five asserted clauses.

  1. By Definition 24, the observed coordinate has \[|\mathcal X| = |\{50,100,150,200,250\}|\, |\{0,31,850\}|^2\,|\{0,1\}| = 5\cdot 3^2\cdot 2 = 90,\] and the latent diet-memory coordinate has \[|\mathcal H|=|\{0,78\}|^2=2^2=4.\] Thus the joint-state alphabet \(\mathcal S=\mathcal X\times\mathcal H\) has \[|\mathcal S|=90\cdot4=360.\]

  2. For a memoryless policy \(p\in\{b,e\}\), let \(P_p^{(T)}:\mathcal S\times\mathcal S\to[0,1]\) be the reward-marginalized joint-state transition matrix \[P_p^{(T)}(s,s') := \sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}p(a\mid s_X)\, K_T\{(r_{\mathrm{out}},\bar s):\bar s=s'\mid s,a\}, \qquad s,s'\in\mathcal S .\] By the construction in Definition 24, the embedded policy kernel of the refreshed insulin experiment is this matrix. Hence, for every pair of probability laws \(\nu,\nu'\) on \(\mathcal S\), Lemma 1 gives \[\|\nu P_p^{(T)}-\nu'P_p^{(T)}\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \le \frac12\|\nu-\nu'\|_{\mathrm{TV}} .\] Applying this with \(p=b\) and \(p=e\) is exactly Assumption 6 with \(\alpha=1/2\).

  3. The behavior probabilities are \[b(1\mid x)=\frac3{10}, \qquad b(0\mid x)=\frac7{10},\] and the target policy is a probability law with \(0\le e(a\mid x)\le1\). Therefore \[e(1\mid x)\le 1=\frac{10}{3}\,\frac3{10} =\frac{10}{3}b(1\mid x),\] and \[e(0\mid x)\le 1\le \frac73 =\frac{10}{3}\,\frac7{10} =\frac{10}{3}b(0\mid x).\] Thus Assumption 5 holds with \(L=10/3\).

  4. Let \(d_b\) and \(d_e\) be the selected stationary laws of \(P_b^{(T)}\) and \(P_e^{(T)}\). The behavior and target policies in Definition 24 are probability vectors; together with the contraction estimate supplied by Lemma 1, Lemma 2 gives \[d_p(s') = \sum_{s\in\mathcal S}d_p(s)P_p^{(T)}(s,s'), \qquad \sum_{s\in\mathcal S}d_p(s)=1, \qquad d_p(s)\ge0,\] for \(p\in\{b,e\}\) and all \(s'\in\mathcal S\). Write \(S_t\) for the joint state at time \(t\). For \(a\in\{0,1\}\) and \(s,s'\in\mathcal S\), define the nonrefresh action kernel as follows. If \[s=(g,d_1,d_2,x_1,x_2,a_1), \qquad s'=(g',d'_1,d'_2,x'_1,x'_2,a'_1),\] let \(\varphi_{s,a}(g')\) be the probability that the Gaussian clipping-and-rounding rule in Definition 24 produces next glucose value \(g'\), and set this probability to zero when \(g'\) is outside the glucose grid. Let \[\pi_D(0)=\frac45, \qquad \pi_D(78)=\frac15, \qquad \pi_E(0)=\pi_E(31)=\frac25, \qquad \pi_E(850)=\frac15,\] with \(\pi_D\) and \(\pi_E\) equal to zero off their displayed supports. Conditional on the nonrefresh branch and action \(a\), Definition 24 draws the new diet and activity coordinates with laws \(\pi_D\) and \(\pi_E\), shifts the old memory coordinates, and stores the current action. Hence the nonrefresh probability of moving from \(s\) to \(s'\) under action \(a\) is \[\omega_a(s,s') := \varphi_{s,a}(g')\,\pi_D(d'_1)\,\pi_E(x'_1)\, \mathbf 1\{d'_2=d_1\}\, \mathbf 1\{x'_2=x_1\}\, \mathbf 1\{a'_1=a\}.\] All factors in this display are nonnegative, so \(\omega_a(s,s')\ge0\). The refresh branch in Definition 24 has probability \(1/2\) and draws the next full state uniformly from the \(360\)-point set \(\mathcal S\), so its contribution to any fixed next state \(s'\) is \[\frac12\cdot\frac1{360}=\frac1{720}.\] The remaining branch has probability \(1/2\), and the policy-induced kernel averages the action-specific nonrefresh probabilities with weights \(p(a\mid s_X)\). Thus, for every memoryless policy \(p\) on \(\{0,1\}\), \[P_p^{(T)}(s,s') = \frac1{720} + \frac12\sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}p(a\mid s_X)\omega_a(s,s'), \qquad s,s'\in\mathcal S .\] Since the behavior policy has nonnegative action probabilities, this identity gives the pointwise minorization \[P_b^{(T)}(s,s') = \frac1{720} +\frac12\sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}b(a\mid s_X)\omega_a(s,s') \ge \frac1{720}, \qquad s,s'\in\mathcal S .\] The stationary-minorization calculation is then \[d_b(s') = \sum_{s\in\mathcal S}d_b(s)P_b^{(T)}(s,s') \ge \sum_{s\in\mathcal S}d_b(s)\frac1{720} = \frac1{720}, \qquad s'\in\mathcal S .\] Since \(d_e\) is a probability vector, \[d_e(s')\le\sum_{\bar s\in\mathcal S}d_e(\bar s)=1.\] Combining the last two displays yields \[d_e(s')\le 1\le 720\,d_b(s'), \qquad s'\in\mathcal S,\] and \(720\ge1\). This proves Assumption 7 with \(C=720\).

  5. For the same horizon \(T\), write \(K\) for the transition kernel of \(\mathcal I_{\mathrm{grid}}\), \(b\) and \(e\) for its behavior and target policies, and \(d_b\) for the stationary law induced by \(b\). The stationary immediate-weighting bias diagnostic is \[\Delta_{\mathrm{imm}}(T) = \sum_s d_b(s) \sum_{a\in\{0,1\}} b(a\mid s_X)\, \frac{e(a\mid s_X)}{b(a\mid s_X)} \int r\,K(ds',dr\mid s,a) - \theta(K,e).\] For this diagnostic on the refreshed finite insulin adaptation \(\mathcal I_{\mathrm{grid}}\) of Definition 24, Lemma 7 supplies the interval certificate \[-\frac6{1000} < \Delta_{\mathrm{imm}}(T) < -\frac{58}{10000},\] which is the displayed bound \[-0.006<\Delta_{\mathrm{imm}}(T)<-0.0058.\]

The five displayed conclusions are precisely the conjunction asserted for the refreshed insulin experiment.

∎
